## I — Blackmail / 第一章　敲诈

===Original===

Halfway along the north side of the main street of Highmarket an ancient stone gateway, imposing enough to suggest that it was originally the entrance to some castellated mansion or manor house, gave access to a square yard, flanked about by equally ancient buildings. What those buildings had been used for in other days was not obvious to the casual and careless observer, but to the least observant their present use was obvious enough. Here were piles of timber from Norway; there were stacks of slate from Wales; here was marble from Aberdeen, and there cement from Portland: the old chambers of the grey buildings were filled to overflowing with all the things that go towards making a house﻿—ironwork, zinc, lead, tiles, great coils of piping, stores of domestic appliances. And on a shining brass plate, set into the wall, just within the gateway, were deeply engraven the words: **Mallalieu and Cotherstone, Builders and Contractors**.

Whoever had walked into Mallalieu & Cotherstone’s yard one October afternoon a few years ago would have seen Mallalieu and Cotherstone in person. The two partners had come out of their office and gone down the yard to inspect half a dozen new carts, just finished, and now drawn up in all the glory of fresh paint. Mallalieu had designed those carts himself, and he was now pointing out their advantages to Cotherstone, who was more concerned with the bookkeeping and letter-writing side of the business than with its actual work. He was a big, fleshy man, Mallalieu, midway between fifty and sixty, of a large, solemn, well-satisfied countenance, small, sly eyes, and an expression of steady watchfulness; his attire was always of the eminently respectable sort, his linen fresh and glossy; the thick gold chain across his ample front, and the silk hat which he invariably wore, gave him an unmistakable air of prosperity. He stood now, the silk hat cocked a little to one side, one hand under the tail of his broadcloth coat, a pudgy finger of the other pointing to some new feature of the mechanism of the new carts, and he looked the personification of self-satisfaction and smug content.

“All done in one action, d’ye see, Cotherstone?” he was saying. “One pull at that pin releases the entire load. We’d really ought to have a patent for that idea.”

Cotherstone went nearer the cart which they were examining. He was a good deal of a contrast to his partner﻿—a slightly built, wiry man, nervous and quick of movement; although he was Mallalieu’s junior he looked older, and the thin hair at his temples was already whitening. Mallalieu suggested solidity and almost bovine sleekness; in Cotherstone, activity of speech and gesture was marked well-nigh to an appearance of habitual anxiety. He stepped about the cart with the quick action of an inquisitive bird or animal examining something which it has never seen before.

===Chinese===

在海马基特（Highmarket）主街北侧的半道上，有一道古旧的石砌门楼，气派不小，足以叫人推想它原是哪座带雉堞的宅邸或庄园府第的大门；从这道门进去，便是一方院落，四下里围着同样古旧的房屋。这些屋子早年间派过什么用场，漫不经心的过路人瞧不出个所以然；可它们如今的用途，便是最不长眼睛的人也一望而知。这边是一垛垛从挪威运来的木料，那边是一摞摞威尔士产的石板瓦；这里有阿伯丁的大理石，那里有波特兰的水泥：灰楼里那些旧库房装得满满当当、几乎外溢，全是造屋盖房用得着的东西——铁活、锌板、铅皮、瓦片、一盘盘粗大的管材，还有成批的家用器具。就在门楼里侧的墙上嵌着一块锃亮的铜牌，上头深深錾着一行字：**马拉柳与科瑟斯通，建筑商与承包商**。

若是几年前十月里的一个下午，有谁走进马拉柳与科瑟斯通商行的院子，准能亲眼见到马拉柳（Mallalieu）和科瑟斯通（Cotherstone）本人。这两位合伙人刚从办公室出来，下到院子深处，查看六辆新造好的运货马车——方才完工，此刻一字排开，新漆油光锃亮，好不威风。这些车是马拉柳亲自设计的，他正指给科瑟斯通看其中的种种好处；后者操心的是行里记账写信那一摊事，实际的营生倒不大过问。马拉柳生得身材魁伟、脑满肠肥，年纪五十五六，一张阔脸盘庄重而自得，一双细小的眼睛透着狡黠，神情里始终带着一分不松懈的提防；他的衣着向来极其体面，衬衣浆洗得挺括光鲜；横在宽阔胸前的粗金链，加上那顶须臾不离头的大礼帽，给他平添了一副毋庸置疑的殷实气派。此刻他站在那里，大礼帽微微歪向一边，一只手拢在呢面礼服的下摆底下，另一只手的短粗指头点着新车机关上的某一处新巧思，那副模样，活脱脱是自鸣得意、沾沾自喜的化身。

“一拉就成，你瞧，科瑟斯通？”他正说着，“把那个销子一拉，整车的货就全卸下来了。这个点子，咱们真该去给它弄个专利。”

科瑟斯通朝他们端详的那辆车凑近了些。他同他的合伙人恰成对照——身量瘦小，筋骨结实，举止急促而带些神经质；论年纪他比马拉柳轻，看上去反倒更老，两鬓的稀发已经见了白。马拉柳通身透着敦实，油光水滑得几乎带着牛相；科瑟斯通则是言语手势频频不断，急切得近乎一副惯常焦灼的样子。他绕着那辆车疾步转来转去，活像一只好奇的鸟儿或小兽，在察看一件生平未见的物事。

===Original===

“Yes, yes, yes!” he answered. “Yes, that’s a good idea. But if it’s to be patented, you know, we ought to see to it at once, before these carts go into use.”

“Why, there’s nobody in Highmarket like to rob us,” observed Mallalieu, good-humouredly. “You might consider about getting﻿—what do they call it?﻿—provisional protection?﻿—for it.”

“I’ll look it up,” responded Cotherstone. “It’s worth that, anyhow.”

“Do,” said Mallalieu. He pulled out the big gold watch which hung from the end of his cable chain and glanced at its jewelled dial. “Dear me!” he exclaimed. “Four o’clock﻿—I’ve a meeting in the Mayor’s parlour at ten past. But I’ll look in again before going home.”

He hurried away towards the entrance gate, and Cotherstone, after ruminative inspection of the new carts, glanced at some papers in his hand and went over to a consignment of goods which required checking. He was carefully ticking them off on a list when a clerk came down the yard.

===Chinese===

“是，是，是！”他答道，“是，这主意不错。不过真要申请专利的话，你晓得，咱们就得赶紧去办，赶在这些车投入使用之前。”

“嗨，海马基特还能有谁来偷咱的点子？”马拉柳和颜悦色地说，“不过你也不妨琢磨琢磨，去办一个——他们管那叫什么来着？——临时保护？——就为这东西办一份。”

“我去查一查，”科瑟斯通应道，“不管怎样，这总归值得。”

“查吧。”马拉柳说。他从粗金链的末端拽出那块大金表，瞥了一眼镶宝石的表盘。“哎呀呀！”他失声道，“都四点了——四点十分我在市长会客室还有个会。不过回家前我还会再进来一趟。”

他急匆匆朝大门楼去了；科瑟斯通对着那几辆新车又沉吟打量了一阵，才看了看手里的几页单子，走向一批待点验的货物。他正对着货单逐件仔细勾核，一个职员顺着院子走了过来。

===Original===

“Mr. Kitely called to pay his rent, sir,” he announced. “He asked to see you yourself.”

“Twenty-five﻿—six﻿—seven,” counted Cotherstone. “Take him into the private office, Stoner,” he answered. “I’ll be there in a minute.”

He continued his checking until it was finished, entered the figures on his list, and went briskly back to the countinghouse near the gateway. There he bustled into a room kept sacred to himself and Mallalieu, with a cheery greeting to his visitor﻿—an elderly man who had recently rented from him a small house on the outskirts of the town.

“Afternoon, Mr. Kitely,” he said. “Glad to see you, sir﻿—always glad to see anybody with a bit of money, eh? Take a chair, sir﻿—I hope you’re satisfied with the little place, Mr. Kitely?”

===Chinese===

“基特利（Kitely）先生来交房租了，先生，”他禀报道，“他指名要见您本人。”

“二十五——二十六——二十七。”科瑟斯通数着数，应道，“请他到里间办公室去坐，斯通纳（Stoner）。我一分钟就到。”

他把手头的点验做完，把数目填进单子，便脚下轻快地折回门楼近旁的账房。他一阵风似的走进那间专为他与马拉柳保留、旁人不得擅入的房间，朝来客热络地道了声好——来客是位老先生，新近才向他租下镇郊的一所小宅子。

“下午好，基特利先生，”他说，“见到您真高兴，先生——凡是身上带着几个钱的人来，见着总是高兴的，是不是？请坐，先生——那所小宅子您住得还满意吧，基特利先生？”

===Original===

The visitor took the offered elbow-chair, folded his hands on the top of his old-fashioned walking-cane, and glanced at his landlord with a half-humorous, half-quizzical expression. He was an elderly, clean-shaven, grey-haired man, spare of figure, dressed in rusty black; a wisp of white neckcloth at his throat gave him something of a clerical appearance: Cotherstone, who knew next to nothing about him, except that he was able to pay his rent and taxes, had already set him down as a retired verger of some cathedral.

“I should think you and Mr. Mallalieu are in no need of a bit of money, Mr. Cotherstone,” he said quietly. “Business seems to be good with you, sir.”

“Oh, so-so,” replied Cotherstone, offhandedly. “Naught to complain of, of course. I’ll give you a receipt, Mr. Kitely,” he went on, seating himself at his desk and taking up a book of forms. “Let’s see﻿—twenty-five pounds a year is six pound five a quarter﻿—there you are, sir. Will you have a drop of whisky?”

Kitely laid a handful of gold and silver on the desk, took the receipt, and nodded his head, still watching Cotherstone with the same half-humorous expression.

===Chinese===

来客在让给他的扶手椅上坐了，双手交叠拄着老式手杖的杖头，带着半是诙谐、半是打趣的神气朝房东瞥了一眼。他是位上了年纪的先生，面庞剃得干干净净，头发花白，身形清瘦，一身旧得发乌的黑衣裳；喉间系着一缕白色颈巾，给他添了几分教士的气派：科瑟斯通对他的来历几乎一无所知，只知道他房租税款一概付得爽利，早就在心里把他认作某座大教堂退下来的教堂司事（verger）了。

“依我看，您和马拉柳先生可不缺这几个钱，科瑟斯通先生，”他安安静静地说，“您的生意瞧着兴旺得很哪，先生。”

“哦，还过得去，”科瑟斯通随口应道，“自然，没啥可抱怨的。我给您开张收据，基特利先生，”他接着说，一边在写字台前坐下，拿起一本票据簿。“让我算算——一年二十五镑，一个季度就是六镑五——您拿好，先生。要不要来一滴威士忌？”

基特利把一把金币银币摊在桌上，接过收据，点了点头，仍旧带着那副半是诙谐的神气，端详着科瑟斯通。

===Original===

“Thank you,” he said. “I shouldn’t mind.”

He watched Cotherstone produce a decanter and glasses, watched him fetch fresh water from a filter in the corner of the room, watched him mix the drinks, and took his own with no more than a polite nod of thanks. And Cotherstone, murmuring an expression of good wishes, took a drink himself, and sat down with his desk-chair turned towards his visitor.

“Aught you’d like doing at the house, Mr. Kitely?” he asked.

“No,” answered Kitely, “no, I can’t say that there is.”

There was something odd, almost taciturn, in his manner, and Cotherstone glanced at him a little wonderingly.

“And how do you like Highmarket, now you’ve had a spell of it?” he inquired. “Got settled down, I suppose, now?”

===Chinese===

“谢谢，”他说，“来一点也无妨。”

他瞧着科瑟斯通端出酒瓶和酒杯，瞧着他从屋角的滤水器里接了干净的凉水，瞧着他把酒对好，接过自己那一杯，不过客气地点头道了声谢。科瑟斯通低低说了句祝愿的话，自己也呷了一口，便把写字台前的椅子转过来朝着来客，坐下了。

“宅子里可有什么要修要补的，基特利先生？”他问。

“没有，”基特利答道，“没有，说不上有什么。”

他的做派里有点说不出的古怪，近乎缄默，科瑟斯通不由得略带诧异地瞅了他一眼。

“您在海马基特也住了一阵子了，觉得这地方怎么样？”他问道，“如今想必是安顿下来了？”

===Original===

“It’s all that I expected,” replied Kitely. “Quiet﻿—peaceful. How do you like it?”

“Me!” exclaimed Cotherstone, surprised. “Me?﻿—why, I’ve had﻿—yes, five-and-twenty years of it!”

Kitely took another sip from his glass and set it down. He gave Cotherstone a sharp look.

“Yes,” he said, “yes﻿—five-and-twenty years. You and your partner, both. Yes﻿—it’ll be just about thirty years since I first saw you. But﻿—you’ve forgotten.”

Cotherstone, who had been lounging forward, warming his hands at the fire, suddenly sat straight up in his chair. His face, always sharp seemed to grow sharper as he turned to his visitor with a questioning look.

===Chinese===

“跟我先前指望的一模一样，”基特利答道，“清静——安逸。您呢，您觉得这儿怎么样？”

“我！”科瑟斯通吃了一惊，叫了起来，“我？——嗨，我在这儿都待了——可不，二十五年啦！”

基特利又从杯里呷了一口，把杯子放下。他锐利地看了科瑟斯通一眼。

“是啊，”他说，“是啊——二十五年。您和您的合伙人，两位都是。是啊——打我头一回见您，算来也刚好三十年了。可是——您忘了。”

科瑟斯通原本俯身坐着，正朝着炉火烘手，这时猛地一下在椅子里坐得笔直。他转向来客，满脸疑问；那张本就尖削的脸，此刻似乎愈发尖削了。

===Original===

“Since﻿—what?” he demanded.

“Since I first saw you﻿—and Mr. Mallalieu,” replied Kitely. “As I say, you’ve forgotten. But﻿—I haven’t.”

Cotherstone sat staring at his tenant for a full minute of speechlessness. Then he slowly rose, walked over to the door, looked at it to see that it was closed, and returning to the hearth, fixed his eyes on Kitely.

“What do you mean?” he asked.

“Just what I say,” answered Kitely, with a dry laugh. “It’s thirty years since I first saw you and Mallalieu. That’s all.”

===Chinese===

“从——从什么时候？”他质问道。

“打我头一回见到您——还有马拉柳先生——的时候，”基特利答道，“我说了，您忘了。可是——我没忘。”

科瑟斯通坐在那里，一言不发，直勾勾地瞪着他的房客，足足瞪了一分钟。随后他缓缓起身，走到门边，看了看门确实关严了，才回到炉边，把两眼死死盯住基特利。

“你这是什么意思？”他问。

“就是我说出口的意思，”基特利答道，干笑一声，“三十年前，我就见过您和马拉柳。如此而已。”

===Original===

“Where?” demanded Cotherstone.

Kitely motioned his landlord to sit down. And Cotherstone sat down﻿—trembling. His arm shook when Kitely laid a hand on it.

“Do you want to know where?” he asked, bending close to Cotherstone. “I’ll tell you. In the dock﻿—at Wilchester Assizes. Eh?”

Cotherstone made no answer. He had put the tips of his fingers together, and now he was tapping the nails of one hand against the nails of the other. And he stared and stared at the face so close to his own﻿—as if it had been the face of a man resurrected from the grave. Within him there was a feeling of extraordinary physical sickness; it was quickly followed by one of inertia, just as extraordinary. He felt as if he had been mesmerized; as if he could neither move nor speak. And Kitely sat there, a hand on his victim’s arm, his face sinister and purposeful, close to his.

===Chinese===

“在哪儿？”科瑟斯通逼问道。

基特利摆了摆手，示意房东坐下。科瑟斯通便坐下了——浑身发颤。基特利一只手搭上他的胳膊，那条胳膊便抖个不住。

“您想知道在哪儿？”他俯身凑近科瑟斯通，问道，“我这就告诉您。在被告席上——在威尔切斯特（Wilchester）巡回法庭上。怎么样？”

科瑟斯通没有作声。他把指尖对指尖地拢在一处，这会儿正用一只手的指甲轻轻叩着另一只手的指甲。他盯着那张近在咫尺的脸，盯了又盯——仿佛那是一张从坟茔里还魂归来的人的脸。他胸腹间涌起一阵异乎寻常的恶心欲呕；紧接着又是一阵同样异乎寻常的浑身乏力。他觉得自己像是被摄了魂魄——像是动也动不得，话也说不出。基特利就坐在那里，一只手按在猎物的胳膊上，那张脸阴森而志在必得，紧贴着他自己的脸。

===Original===

“Fact!” he murmured. “Absolute fact! I remember everything. It’s come on me bit by bit, though. I thought I knew you when I first came here﻿—then I had a feeling that I knew Mallalieu. And﻿—in time﻿—I remembered﻿—everything! Of course, when I saw you both﻿—where I did see you﻿—you weren’t Mallalieu & Cotherstone. You were﻿—”

Cotherstone suddenly made an effort, and shook off the thin fingers which lay on his sleeve. His pale face grew crimson, and the veins swelled on his forehead.

“Confound you!” he said in a low, concentrated voice. “Who are you?”

Kitely shook his head and smiled quietly.

“No need to grow warm,” he answered. “Of course, it’s excusable in you. Who am I? Well, if you really want to know, I’ve been employed in the police line for thirty-five years﻿—until lately.”

===Chinese===

“是事实！”他喃喃道，“千真万确的事实！我什么都记得。虽说是零零碎碎、一点一点涌上来的。我刚到这儿来的时候，就觉得您面熟——跟着又觉得马拉柳也面熟。再后来——时候一到——我想起来了——全想起来了！当然喽，我见到你们二位的那一回——在我见到你们的那个地方——你们还不叫马拉柳与科瑟斯通。你们二位叫——”

科瑟斯通猛地一使劲，甩开了搭在衣袖上的那几根干瘦手指。他苍白的脸涨得通红，额头上的青筋根根绽起。

“该死的！”他声音低沉、字字用劲地说，“你到底是什么人？”

基特利摇了摇头，安然地微微一笑。

“犯不上动这么大的火气，”他答道，“当然啦，您这般沉不住气也情有可原。我是什么人？嗯——您要是真想知道，我在警察这一行当了三十五年的差——直到不久之前。”

===Original===

“A detective!” exclaimed Cotherstone.

“Not when I was present at Wilchester﻿—that time,” replied Kitely. “But afterwards﻿—in due course. Ah!﻿—do you know, I often was curious as to what became of you both! But I never dreamed of meeting you﻿—here. Of course, you came up North after you’d done your time? Changed your names, started a new life﻿—and here you are! Clever!”

Cotherstone was recovering his wits. He had got out of his chair by that time, and had taken up a position on the hearthrug, his back to the fire, his hands in his pockets, his eyes on his visitor. He was thinking﻿—and for the moment he let Kitely talk.

===Chinese===

“侦探！”科瑟斯通失声叫道。

“我上威尔切斯特去的那一回——那时候——还谈不上，”基特利答道，“那是后来——顺理成章，水到渠成。啊！——您可知道，我常常纳闷，你们俩后来到底落了个什么下场！可我做梦也想不到，会在这儿撞见您。当然喽，你们服满了刑就北上了，对不对？改了名姓，重起炉灶——就有了今天这番光景！高明！”

科瑟斯通渐渐回过神来。这当儿他已从椅子里起身，在炉前地毯上站定，背对着炉火，双手插在口袋里，眼睛盯着来客。他心里正打着算盘——眼下且由着基特利说去。

===Original===

“Yes﻿—clever!” continued Kitely in the same level, subdued tones, “very clever indeed! I suppose you’d carefully planted some of that money you﻿—got hold of? Must have done, of course﻿—you’d want money to start this business. Well, you’ve done all this on the straight, anyhow. And you’ve done well, too. Odd, isn’t it, that I should come to live down here, right away in the far North of England, and find you in such good circumstances, too! Mr. Mallalieu, Mayor of Highmarket﻿—his second term of office! Mr. Cotherstone, Borough Treasurer of Highmarket﻿—now in his sixth year of that important post! I say again﻿—you’ve both done uncommonly well﻿—uncommonly!”

“Have you got any more to say?” asked Cotherstone.

But Kitely evidently intended to say what he had to say in his own fashion. He took no notice of Cotherstone’s question, and presently, as if he were amusing himself with reminiscences of a long dead past, he spoke again, quietly and slowly.

===Chinese===

“是啊——高明！”基特利接着说，还是那种平平的、压低了的声调，“实在高明得很！我料想，你们从那笔——弄到手的钱里，早细心存下了一份吧？必定是存下了——不这样，你们哪来的本钱开这份买卖。嗯，好歹这一切你们是走正道挣出来的。而且挣得着实不赖。说来也怪，是不是？我大老远跑到英格兰顶北边来落脚，居然还能撞见你们这样家业兴旺！马拉柳先生，海马基特市长——第二届任期！科瑟斯通先生，海马基特市政财政官（Borough Treasurer）——这个要紧职位，如今已是第六个年头！我再说一遍——你们两位发达得实在不寻常——不寻常！”

“你还有别的要说的没有？”科瑟斯通问。

可基特利分明打定了主意，要照他自己的路数把话说完。科瑟斯通这一问，他只当没听见；过了一会儿，他仿佛正自得其乐地追怀着一段久已死去的往事，又开了口，声气又轻又慢。

===Original===

“Yes,” he murmured, “uncommonly well! And of course you’d have capital. Put safely away, of course, while you were doing your time. Let’s see﻿—it was a Building Society that you defrauded, wasn’t it? Mallalieu was treasurer, and you were secretary. Yes﻿—I remember now. The amount was two thous﻿—”

Cotherstone made a sudden exclamation and a sharp movement﻿—both checked by an equally sudden change of attitude and expression on the part of the ex-detective. For Kitely sat straight up and looked the junior partner squarely in the face.

“Better not, Mr. Cotherstone!” he said, with a grin that showed his yellow teeth. “You can’t very well choke the life out of me in your own office, can you? You couldn’t hide my old carcase as easily as you and Mallalieu hid those Building Society funds, you know. So﻿—be calm! I’m a reasonable man﻿—and getting an old man.”

===Chinese===

“是啊，”他喃喃道，“发达得不寻常！当然，你们手里还有本钱。稳稳当当存着的，自然——趁你们坐牢的工夫。让我想想——你们坑的是一家建房互助会（Building Society），对不对？马拉柳当司库，您当秘书。是了——我这会儿想起来了。那笔款项的数目是两千——”

科瑟斯通蓦地叫出声来，蓦地扑动了一下——这两下都被这位退职侦探同样陡然的一变姿态、一变脸色镇住了。只见基特利霍地坐直身子，逼视着这位年轻合伙人的脸。

“最好别乱来，科瑟斯通先生！”他说着，咧嘴一笑，露出一口黄牙，“在您自己的办公室里把我掐死，怕不怎么妥当吧？您总不能把我这把老骨头藏起来，藏得像您和马拉柳藏那笔建房互助会的款子一样干净利落，您说是吧。所以——放镇静些！我是个讲道理的人——再说，也一天天老啦。”

===Original===

He accompanied the last words with a meaning smile, and Cotherstone took a turn or two about the room, trying to steady himself. And Kitely presently went on again, in the same monotonous tones:

“Think it all out﻿—by all means,” he said. “I don’t suppose there’s a soul in all England but myself knows your secret﻿—and Mallalieu’s. It was sheer accident, of course, that I ever discovered it. But﻿—I know! Just consider what I do know. Consider, too, what you stand to lose. There’s Mallalieu, so much respected that he’s Mayor of this ancient borough for the second time. There’s you﻿—so much trusted that you’ve been Borough Treasurer for years. You can’t afford to let me tell the Highmarket folk that you two are ex-convicts! Besides, in your case there’s another thing﻿—there’s your daughter.”

Cotherstone groaned﻿—a deep, unmistakable groan of sheer torture. But Kitely went on remorselessly.

“Your daughter’s just about to marry the most promising young man in the place,” he said. “A young fellow with a career before him. Do you think he’d marry her if he knew that her father﻿—even if it is thirty years ago﻿—had been convicted of﻿—”

===Chinese===

末了这几句话，他配上了一抹意味深长的微笑；科瑟斯通在屋里来回踱了两趟，竭力让自己稳住。基特利随即又说了下去，还是那副单调的声调：

“好好盘算去吧——尽管盘算，”他说，“我估摸着，偌大一个英格兰，除了我，再没有一个活人知道您的秘密——还有马拉柳的秘密。我当初察觉这桩事，纯属撞了大运，那自然。可是——我知道！您只消掂量掂量我知道些什么。再掂量掂量您会赔上些什么。一边是马拉柳，德高望重，都当上这座古老自治市第二任市长了。一边是您——众望所归，连着好几年当市政财政官。您可担待不起让我去告诉海马基特人：你们两位是蹲过班房的前科犯！再说，您这儿还另有一层——您有个女儿。”

科瑟斯通呻吟了一声——一声深沉的、一听便知的、酷刑熬煎般的呻吟。基特利却毫无怜惜地继续说下去。

“您的千金，眼下正要许配给本地最有前程的年轻人，”他说，“一个小伙子，前头摆着大好的前程。您想，他若是知道了她的父亲——哪怕那是三十年前的旧事——曾经被判过——”

===Original===

“Look you here!” interrupted Cotherstone, through set teeth. “I’ve had enough! I’ve asked you once before if you’d any more to say﻿—now I’ll put it in another fashion. For I see what you’re after﻿—and it’s blackmail! How much do you want? Come on﻿—give it a name!”

“Name nothing, till you’ve told Mallalieu,” answered Kitely. “There’s no hurry. You two can’t, and I shan’t, run away. Time enough﻿—I’ve the whip hand. Tell your partner, the Mayor, all I’ve told you﻿—then you can put your heads together, and see what you’re inclined to do. An annuity, now?﻿—that would suit me.”

“You haven’t mentioned this to a soul?” asked Cotherstone anxiously.

“Bah!” sneered Kitely. “D’ye think I’m a fool? Not likely. Well﻿—now you know. I’ll come in here again tomorrow afternoon. And﻿—you’ll both be here, and ready with a proposal.”

He picked up his glass, leisurely drank off its remaining contents, and without a word of farewell opened the door and went quietly away.

===Chinese===

“你给我听着！”科瑟斯通咬紧牙关打断了他，“我受够了！先前我问过你一句，还有没有别的话——如今我换个问法。因为我看穿了你打的什么主意——这是敲诈！你要多少？痛快些——开个价！”

“先别忙着开价，等您告诉了马拉柳再说，”基特利答道，“不着急。你们两位跑不掉，我也不会走。有的是工夫——主动权攥在我手里。把我对您说的这些话，原原本本告诉你那位当市长的合伙人——然后你们哥儿俩凑在一处，商量商量打算怎么办。比如，来一笔年金？——那倒正合我的意。”

“这些话，你对一个活人提起过没有？”科瑟斯通焦灼地问。

“呸！”基特利嗤笑一声，“您当我是傻子？才不会呢。好——如今您知道了。明天下午我再到这儿来。到时候——你们两位都得在场，还得备好一个提议。”

他端起酒杯，不紧不慢地饮尽了剩下的酒，一句道别的话也没有，开门出去，悄没声儿地走了。

## II / 第二章

===Original===

Crime﻿—and Success

For some moments after Kitely had left him, Cotherstone stood vacantly staring at the chair in which the blackmailer had sat. As yet he could not realize things. He was only filled with a queer, vague amazement about Kitely himself. He began to look back on his relations with Kitely. They were recent﻿—very recent, only of yesterday, as you might say. Kitely had come to him, one day about three months previously, told him that he had come to these parts for a bit of a holiday, taken a fancy to a cottage which he, Cotherstone, had to let, and inquired its rent. He had mentioned, casually, that he had just retired from business, and wanted a quiet place wherein to spend the rest of his days. He had taken the cottage, and given his landlord satisfactory references as to his ability to pay the rent﻿—and Cotherstone, always a busy man, had thought no more about him. Certainly he had never anticipated such an announcement as that which Kitely had just made to him﻿—never dreamed that Kitely had recognized him and Mallalieu as men he had known thirty years ago.

It had been Cotherstone’s lifelong endeavour to forget all about the event of thirty years ago, and to a large extent he had succeeded in dulling his memory. But Kitely had brought it all back﻿—and now everything was fresh to him. His brows knitted and his face grew dark as he thought of one thing in his past of which Kitely had spoken so easily and glibly﻿—the dock. He saw himself in that dock again﻿—and Mallalieu standing by him. They were not called Mallalieu and Cotherstone then, of course. He remembered what their real names were﻿—he remembered, too, that, until a few minutes before, he had certainly not repeated them, even to himself, for many a long year. Oh, yes﻿—he remembered everything﻿—he saw it all again. The case had excited plenty of attention in Wilchester at the time﻿—Wilchester, that for thirty years had been so far away in thought and in actual distance that it might have been some place in the Antipodes. It was not a nice case﻿—even now, looking back upon it from his present standpoint, it made him blush to think of. Two better-class young workingmen, charged with embezzling the funds of a building society to which they had acted as treasurer and secretary!﻿—a bad case. The Court had thought it a bad case, and the culprits had been sentenced to two years’ imprisonment. And now Cotherstone only remembered that imprisonment as one remembers a particularly bad dream. Yes﻿—it had been real.

His eyes, moody and brooding, suddenly shifted their gaze from the easy chair to his own hands﻿—they were shaking. Mechanically he took up the whisky decanter from his desk, and poured some of its contents into his glass﻿—the rim of the glass tinkled against the neck of the decanter. Yes﻿—that had been a shock, right enough, he muttered to himself, and not all the whisky in the world would drive it out of him. But a drink﻿—neat and stiff﻿—would pull his nerves up to pitch, and so he drank, once, twice, and sat down with the glass in his hand﻿—to think still more.

===Chinese===

罪恶——与成功

基特利（Kitely）走后，科瑟斯通（Cotherstone）兀自站着发了好一会儿怔，直勾勾地盯着那敲诈者方才坐过的椅子。他一时还回不过神来，心里只是对基特利这个人怀着一种怪异的、朦胧的惊诧。他开始回想自己同基特利的交往。那是新近的事——新近得很，简直可以说就是昨天。约莫三个月前的一天，基特利来找他，说自己到这一带来度个短假，看中了他手下一间待租的小屋，便打听租金多少。他还随口提起，自己刚刚歇了生意，想寻个清静地方打发余生。他把小屋租了下来，还向房东出具了令人满意的资信证明，表明自己付得起房租——而科瑟斯通向来是个忙人，过后便没再多想他。他当然万万没有料到基特利方才会说出那番话来——做梦也不曾想到，基特利竟认出了他和马拉柳（Mallalieu），认出这两人正是他三十年前认识的旧相识。

忘掉三十年前那桩事，是科瑟斯通半生一以贯之的努力，而且大体上他也做到了，把记忆磨得迟钝了。可基特利把这一切又全数翻了出来——如今桩桩件件都历历如新。他一想到基特利说得那样轻巧圆熟的旧事之一——被告席——眉头便拧了起来，脸色也阴沉下去。他又看见自己站上那个被告席——马拉柳侍立一旁。当然，那时他们不叫马拉柳和科瑟斯通。他记得两人的真名实姓——他还记得，就在几分钟之前，这些名字他已有许多许多年不曾念起，连对自己都未曾念起过。哦，是的——他什么都记得——一切又在眼前重演。当年这案子在威尔切斯特（Wilchester）轰动一时——威尔切斯特，那地方三十年来无论在心里还是在里程上都远不可及，仿佛是地球背面某处的一隅。那不是一桩体面的案子——即便如今站在此时此地回望，想起来仍叫他面红。两个身处较高阶层的年轻工人，被控贪污侵吞一处建房互助会的公款，而他们俩在这会里担当的正是司库和秘书！——一桩丑案。法庭也认定这是桩丑案，两名案犯各判两年监禁。如今科瑟斯通回想那段牢狱，就像回想一场格外凶险的噩梦。是的——那一切都是真的。

他那双郁郁沉郁的眼睛，忽然把目光从安乐椅移到自己的一双手上——手正在发抖。他机械地拿起书桌上的威士忌酒瓶，往杯子里倒了些酒——杯口磕在瓶颈上，叮的一响。是的——这一惊着实不轻，他喃喃自语，天下的威士忌全倒进来也驱不散它。不过一口酒——纯的、烈的——能把神经重新吊起来，于是他喝了一口，又一口，端着杯子坐下——接着细细思量。

===Original===

That old Kitely was shrewd﻿—shrewd! He had at once hit on a fact which those Wilchester folk of thirty years ago had never suspected. It had been said at the time that the two offenders had lost the building society’s money in gambling and speculation, and there had been grounds for such a belief. But that was not so. Most of the money had been skilfully and carefully put where the two conspirators could lay hands on it as soon as it was wanted, and when the term of imprisonment was over they had nothing to do but take possession of it for their own purposes. They had engineered everything very well﻿—Cotherstone’s essentially constructive mind, regarding their doings from the vantage ground of thirty years’ difference, acknowledged that they had been cute, crafty, and cautious to an admirable degree of perfection. Quietly and unobtrusively they had completely disappeared from their own district in the extreme South of England, when their punishment was over. They had let it get abroad that they were going to another continent, to retrieve the past and start a new life; it was even known that they repaired to Liverpool, to take ship for America. But in Liverpool they had shuffled off everything of the past﻿—names, relations, antecedents. There was no reason why anyone should watch them out of the country, but they had adopted precautions against such watching. They separated, disappeared, met again in the far North, in a sparsely-populated, lonely country of hill and dale, led there by an advertisement which they had seen in a local newspaper, met with by sheer chance in a Liverpool hotel. There was an old-established business to sell as a going concern, in the dale town of Highmarket: the two ex-convicts bought it. From that time they were Anthony Mallalieu and Milford Cotherstone, and the past was dead.

During the thirty years in which that past had been dead, Cotherstone had often heard men remark that this world of ours is a very small one, and he had secretly laughed at them. To him and to his partner the world had been wide and big enough. They were now four hundred miles away from the scene of their crime. There was nothing whatever to bring Wilchester people into that northern country, nothing to take Highmarket folk anywhere near Wilchester. Neither he nor Mallalieu ever went far afield﻿—London they avoided with particular care, lest they should meet anyone there who had known them in the old days. They had stopped at home, and minded their business, year in and year out. Naturally, they had prospered. They had speedily become known as hardworking young men; then as good employers of labour; finally as men of considerable standing in a town of which there were only some five thousand inhabitants. They had been invited to join in public matters﻿—Mallalieu had gone into the Town Council first; Cotherstone had followed him later. They had been as successful in administering the affairs of the little town as in conducting their own, and in time both had attained high honours: Mallalieu was now wearing the mayoral chain for the second time; Cotherstone, as Borough Treasurer, had governed the financial matters of Highmarket for several years. And as he sat there, staring at the red embers of the office fire, he remembered that there were no two men in the whole town who were more trusted and respected than he and his partner﻿—his partner in success﻿ ﻿… and in crime.

But that was not all. Both men had married within a few years of their coming to Highmarket. They had married young women of good standing in the neighbourhood; it was perhaps well, reflected Cotherstone, that their wives were dead, and that Mallalieu had never been blessed with children. But Cotherstone had a daughter, of whom he was as fond as he was proud; for her he had toiled and contrived, always intending her to be a rich woman. He had seen to it that she was well educated; he had even allowed himself to be deprived of her company for two years while she went to an expensive school, far away; since she had grown up, he had surrounded her with every comfort. And now, as Kitely had reminded him, she was engaged to be married to the most promising young man in Highmarket, Windle Bent, a rich manufacturer, who had succeeded to and greatly developed a fine business, who had already made his mark on the Town Council, and was known to cherish Parliamentary ambitions. Everybody knew that Bent had a big career before him; he had all the necessary gifts; all the proper stuff in him for such a career. He would succeed; he would probably win a title for himself﻿—a baronetcy, perhaps a peerage. This was just the marriage which Cotherstone desired for Lettie; he would die more than happy if he could once hear her called Your Ladyship. And now here was﻿—this!

===Chinese===

这个老基特利精明——精明得很！他一语点破了一桩事实，而三十年前威尔切斯特那些人对此从未有过半分怀疑。当年人们纷纷议论，说两名案犯把建房互助会的钱在赌桌和投机生意上败光了，这话也确有几分依据。但实情并非如此。那笔钱的大半，早已被两个同谋者手法灵巧、步步为营地安放妥当，什么时候要用，什么时候就能到手；刑期一满，他们只须把它收归己用。他们把一切都布置得极好——科瑟斯通那颗天生长于营构的头脑，站在三十年后的高处回望两人的作为，也只得承认他们干得机灵、狡黠、谨慎，精熟到了令人叹服的地步。服刑期满之后，他们悄没声息、不引人注目地从英格兰极南部的故土彻底消失了。他们放出风声，说要远走另一块大陆，去弥补过往、另起炉灶重过日子；甚至有人确知他们到了利物浦（Liverpool），要搭船去美洲。可就在利物浦，他们把过去的一切抖落了个干净——姓名、亲族、来历。其实并没有任何理由会有人盯着他们出境，他们却仍防了这一手。两人分头走，各自隐没，又在遥远的北方重新聚首——那是一片人烟稀少、丘谷寂寥的乡野；引他们前去的是本地报纸上一则广告，而那份报纸，是他们纯属偶然在利物浦一家旅馆里撞见的。谷地小镇海马基特（Highmarket）有一家老字号商号连铺带生意一并出让：两个坐过牢的人把它盘了下来。自那时起，他们便是安东尼·马拉柳（Anthony Mallalieu）和米尔福德·科瑟斯通（Milford Cotherstone），过去就此死了。

在那过去死去的三十个年头里，科瑟斯通常听人感叹这个世界实在太小，他都在心里暗笑。对他和他的合伙人来说，这世界够宽、够大。他们如今远在罪案发生地四百英里之外。没有任何因由会把威尔切斯特人带进这片北国乡土，也没有任何因由会让海马基特人走到威尔切斯特左近。他和马拉柳从不远行——伦敦（London）更是刻意避着，唯恐在那里撞见认得他们旧时模样的人。他们守在家里，年复一年只管做生意，自然也就兴旺发达了。他们先是很快得了个勤勉后生的名声，继而成了体恤雇工的好东家，末了在这座统共五千上下居民的小镇上，成了数得上号的人物。他们被邀参与公共事务——马拉柳先进了镇议会，科瑟斯通随后跟上。他们治理小镇公务同打理自家生意一样在行，日久天长，两人都挣得了显耀的荣衔：马拉柳如今第二次挂上了市长的链徽；科瑟斯通身为市政财政官（Borough Treasurer），执掌海马基特财政已有好些年头。他坐在那儿，盯着办公室炉火里的红炭，想起全镇上下再没有哪两个人比他和他的合伙人更受信任、更受敬重——同他共享成功的合伙人……也共享罪恶的合伙人。

可还不止于此。两人来到海马基特不出几年便都娶了亲，娶的都是左近一带有身份人家的年轻姑娘。科瑟斯通暗自思忖，两位妻子如今都已亡故，而马拉柳又从未蒙上天赐子，这或许倒是幸事。但科瑟斯通有个女儿，他疼她，也为她骄傲；他为她操劳经营，一心要她做个有钱人。他打点她受了良好的教育，甚至舍得割舍两年骨肉相伴，送她去很远的一所昂贵的学校念书；她长大成人之后，他更让她锦衣玉食、诸事周全。而现在，正如基特利提醒他的，她已经订了婚，许给了海马基特最有前程的年轻人温德尔·本特（Windle Bent）——一位富有的制造商，继承了一桩好生意又将其大为光大，在镇议会里已经崭露头角，况且人人皆知他胸中怀有进身议会的抱负。谁都知道本特前程远大；他样样才具齐备，骨子里正是成就那番事业的好材料。他会成功的；多半还能为自己挣得一个头衔——从男爵，说不定是贵族爵位。这正是科瑟斯通想替莱蒂（Lettie）攀下的亲事；倘若能有朝一日听见旁人唤她一声“夫人”，他死也甘愿，甘愿得不能再甘愿了。可现在却来了——这么一桩事！

===Original===

Cotherstone sat there a long time, thinking, reflecting, reckoning up things. The dusk had come; the darkness followed; he made no movement towards the gas bracket. Nothing mattered but his trouble. That must be dealt with. At all costs, Kitely’s silence must be purchased﻿—aye, even if it cost him and Mallalieu one-half of what they had. And, of course, Mallalieu must be told﻿—at once.

A tap of somebody’s knuckles on the door of the private room roused him at last, and he sprang up and seized a box of matches as he bade the person without to enter. The clerk came in, carrying a sheaf of papers, and Cotherstone bustled to the gas.

“Dear me!” he exclaimed. “I’ve dropped off into a nod over this warm fire, Stoner. What’s that﻿—letters?”

“There’s all these letters to sign, Mr. Cotherstone, and these three contracts to go through,” answered the clerk. “And there are those specifications to examine, as well.”

“Mr. Mallalieu’ll have to see those,” said Cotherstone. He lighted the gas above his desk, put the decanter and the glasses aside, and took the letters. “I’ll sign these, anyhow,” he said, “and then you can post ’em as you go home. The other papers’ll do tomorrow morning.”

The clerk stood slightly behind his master as Cotherstone signed one letter after the other, glancing quickly through each. He was a young man of twenty-two or three, with quick, observant manners, a keen eye, and a not handsome face, and as he stood there the face was bent on Cotherstone with a surmising look. Stoner had noticed his employer’s thoughtful attitude, the gloom in which Cotherstone sat, the decanter on the table, the glass in Cotherstone’s hand, and he knew that Cotherstone was telling a fib when he said he had been asleep. He noticed, too, the six sovereigns and the two or three silver coins lying on the desk, and he wondered what had made his master so abstracted that he had forgotten to pocket them. For he knew Cotherstone well, and Cotherstone was so particular about money that he never allowed even a penny to lie out of place.

===Chinese===

科瑟斯通在那里坐了许久，思着，想着，把一件件事掂量盘算。暮色降临，黑夜接踵而至；他一动也不动，不去理那煤气灯吊架。什么都无关紧要，唯独这桩祸事要紧。这事必须了断。无论如何也要买下基特利的沉默——是啊，哪怕代价是他和马拉柳让出一半家财。当然，还得把这事告诉马拉柳——立刻就告诉。

终于，有人在里间门上轻轻叩了几下，把他惊动了。他一跃而起，一面吩咐外面的人进来，一面抓起一盒火柴。办事员抱着一沓文件走进来，科瑟斯通忙不迭地走去点煤气灯。

“哎呀呀！”他叫道。“守着这暖烘烘的炉火，我竟打起盹来了，斯通纳（Stoner）。那是什么——信件？”

“这一沓信都等着您签字，科瑟斯通先生，还有这三份合同要过目，”办事员答道。“另外还有那几份规格说明书要审。”

“那些得马拉柳先生过目，”科瑟斯通说。他点亮书桌上方的煤气灯，把酒瓶和酒杯挪到一旁，接过信件。“这些信好歹先签了，”他说，“然后你回家路上把它们寄了。其余的文件明天上午再办。”

科瑟斯通一封接一封地签，办事员略站在他身后，对每封信都飞快扫上一眼。这是个二十二三岁的年轻人，举止敏捷、惯于察言观色，眼神锐利，相貌却算不上俊秀；他站在那里，一张脸正带着揣度的神色向科瑟斯通望过去。斯通纳早已留意到东家那副若有所思的姿态、科瑟斯通枯坐其中的那团阴郁、桌上的酒瓶、他手里的酒杯，心里明白科瑟斯通说自己打盹纯属睁眼说瞎话。他还注意到摊在书桌上的六枚金镑和两三枚银币，暗自诧异是什么事让东家这般神不守舍，竟忘了把钱收进兜里。因为他深知科瑟斯通其人——他对钱财精细到那种地步，连一个便士都不容许搁错了地方。

===Original===

“There!” said Cotherstone, handing back the batch of letters. “You’ll be going now, I suppose. Put those in the post. I’m not going just yet, so I’ll lock up the office. Leave the outer door open﻿—Mr. Mallalieu’s coming back.”

He pulled down the blinds of the private room when Stoner had gone, and that done he fell to walking up and down, awaiting his partner. And presently Mallalieu came, smoking a cigar, and evidently in as good humour as usual.

“Oh, you’re still here?” he said as he entered. “I﻿—what’s up?”

He had come to a sudden halt close to his partner, and he now stood staring at him. And Cotherstone, glancing past Mallalieu’s broad shoulder at a mirror, saw that he himself had become startlingly pale and haggard. He looked twenty years older than he had looked when he shaved himself that morning.

“Aren’t you well?” demanded Mallalieu. “What is it?”

Cotherstone made no answer. He walked past Mallalieu and looked into the outer office. The clerk had gone, and the place was only half-lighted. But Cotherstone closed the door with great care, and when he went back to Mallalieu he sank his voice to a whisper.

===Chinese===

“好了！”科瑟斯通说着，把那沓信递了回去。“我想你这就要走了吧。把这些寄出去。我还不动身，办公室我来锁。外间的门留开着——马拉柳先生要回来。”

斯通纳走后，他拉下了里间的百叶窗；做完这个，他便来回踱步，等候合伙人。不多时，马拉柳来了，叼着一支雪茄，显然一如既往地好兴致。

“哦，你还在？”他进门时说。“我——怎么啦？”

他在合伙人跟前猛地站定，此刻正睁大眼盯着他。科瑟斯通越过马拉柳宽阔的肩膀瞥见一面镜子，看见自己已经变得面色惨白、形容枯槁，骇人得很。比起早晨刮脸时的模样，他看上去老了二十岁。

“你不舒服？”马拉柳追问道。“到底怎么回事？”

科瑟斯通不作声。他从马拉柳身边走过，朝外间张望。办事员已经走了，那里只点半明半暗的灯。科瑟斯通却小心翼翼地掩上门，回到马拉柳跟前时，把嗓音压成了耳语。

===Original===

“Bad news!” he said. “Bad﻿—bad news!”

“What about?” asked Mallalieu. “Private? Business?”

Cotherstone put his lips almost close to Mallalieu’s ear.

“That man Kitely﻿—my new tenant,” he whispered. “He’s met us﻿—you and me﻿—before!”

Mallalieu’s rosy cheeks paled, and he turned sharply on his companion.

“Met﻿—us!” he exclaimed. “Him! Where?﻿—when?”

===Chinese===

“坏消息！”他说。“坏——坏透了的消息！”

“什么事上的？”马拉柳问。“私事？还是公务？”

科瑟斯通把嘴唇几乎贴到马拉柳耳根。

“那个人基特利——我新招的房客，”他悄声道。“他从前见过我们——见过你和我！”

马拉柳红润的双颊刷地褪了色，猛地转身逼视他的同伴。

“见过——我们！”他失声叫道。“他！在哪儿？——什么时候？”

===Original===

Cotherstone got his lips still closer.

“Wilchester!” he answered. “Thirty years ago. He﻿—knows!”

Mallalieu dropped into the nearest chair: dropped as if he had been shot. His face, full of colour from the keen air outside, became as pale as his partner’s; his jaw fell, his mouth opened; a strained look came into his small eyes.

“Gad!” he muttered hoarsely. “You﻿—you don’t say so!”

“It’s a fact,” answered Cotherstone. “He knows everything. He’s an ex-detective. He was there﻿—that day.”

“Tracked us down?” asked Mallalieu. “That it?”

===Chinese===

科瑟斯通把嘴凑得愈发近了。

“威尔切斯特！”他答道。“三十年前。他——知道！”

马拉柳跌进最近的一把椅子：像中了枪似的倒了下去。他那张被户外凛冽空气浸得血色充盈的脸，霎时变得和合伙人一样惨白；下巴垮了，嘴巴张开；那双小眼睛里透出一股紧绷绷的神色。

“天哪！”他嗓音沙哑地喃喃。“你——这话当真？！”

“是事实，”科瑟斯通答道。“他什么都知道。他以前干过侦探。那天——他就在场。”

“是循线追到我们的？”马拉柳问。“是这样？”

===Original===

“No,” said Cotherstone. “Sheer chance﻿—pure accident. Recognized us﻿—after he came here. Aye﻿—after all these years! Thirty years!”

Mallalieu’s eyes, roving about the room, fell on the decanter. He pulled himself out of his chair, found a clean glass, and took a stiff drink. And his partner, watching him, saw that his hands, too, were shaking.

“That’s a facer!” said Mallalieu. His voice had grown stronger, and the colour came back to his cheeks. “A real facer! As you say﻿—after thirty years! It’s hard﻿—it’s blessed hard! And﻿—what does he want? What’s he going to do?”

“Wants to blackmail us, of course,” replied Cotherstone, with a mirthless laugh. “What else should he do? What could he do? Why, he could tell all Highmarket who we are, and﻿—”

“Aye, aye!﻿—but the thing is here,” interrupted Mallalieu.

“Supposing we do square him?﻿—is there any reliance to be placed on him then? It ’ud only be the old game﻿—he’d only want more.”

===Chinese===

“不，”科瑟斯通说。“纯属碰巧——纯粹的偶然。是到这儿以后才认出我们来的。是啊——隔了这么多年！整整三十年！”

马拉柳的目光在屋里四下打转，落到了那只酒瓶上。他从椅子里撑起来，找了一只干净杯子，灌下一大口烈酒。合伙人瞧在眼里，看见他那双手也在打颤。

“这可真是一记迎面闷棍！”马拉柳说。他的嗓门重新壮了起来，脸颊上也回了血色。“结结实实的一记！正如你说的——隔了三十年哪！难受——真真难受啊！还有——他要什么？他打算怎么着？”

“还不是要敲诈我们，”科瑟斯通答道，干笑了一声，全无笑意。“不然他还能干什么？他能干什么？嘿，他可以把我们的底细向全海马基特抖个干净，而且——”

“是啊，是啊！——可问题出在这儿，”马拉柳打断他。

“就算我们花钱把他摆平了？——往后他就靠得住了？无非又是那套老把戏——他只会胃口越来越大。”

===Original===

“He said an annuity,” remarked Cotherstone, thoughtfully. “And he added significantly, that he was getting an old man.”

“How old?” demanded Mallalieu.

“Between sixty and seventy,” said Cotherstone. “I’m under the impression that he could be squared, could be satisfied. He’ll have to be! We can’t let it get out﻿—I can’t, anyway. There’s my daughter to think of.”

“D’ye think I’d let it get out?” asked Mallalieu. “No!﻿—all I’m thinking of is if we really can silence him. I’ve heard of cases where a man’s paid blackmail for years and years, and been no better for it in the end.”

“Well﻿—he’s coming here tomorrow afternoon some time,” said Cotherstone. “We’d better see him﻿—together. After all, a hundred a year﻿—a couple of hundred a year﻿—’ud be better than﻿—exposure.”

===Chinese===

“他要的是一份年金，”科瑟斯通若有所思地说。“他还意味深长地添了一句，说自己渐渐上年纪了。”

“多大年纪？”马拉柳追问。

“六七十岁之间，”科瑟斯通说。“依我看，这人是摆得平的、喂得饱的。也非喂饱不可！这事万不能外泄——反正我不能。我还得为我女儿着想。”

“你以为我就会让它外泄？”马拉柳问。“不会！——我盘算的只是，我们到底能不能真正叫他闭嘴。我听说过这样的例子：有人一年又一年地付敲诈钱，到头来落不着半点好处。”

“这样吧——明天下午什么时候他要到这儿来，”科瑟斯通说。“我们最好一起见他。说到底，一年一百镑——哪怕一年两三百镑——总归比——丑事败露强。”

===Original===

Mallalieu drank off his whisky and pushed the glass aside.

“I’ll consider it,” he remarked. “What’s certain sure is that he’ll have to be quietened. I must go﻿—I’ve an appointment. Are you coming out?”

“Not yet,” replied Cotherstone. “I’ve all these papers to go through. Well, think it well over. He’s a man to be feared.”

Mallalieu made no answer. He, like Kitely, went off without a word of farewell, and Cotherstone was once more left alone.

===Chinese===

马拉柳把杯中威士忌一饮而尽，将杯子推到一边。

“我会斟酌的，”他说。“千真万确的是，非得让他闭嘴不可。我得走了——还有个约。你一块儿出去吗？”

“暂时不走，”科瑟斯通答道。“这一堆文件我还得过目。好了，你仔细掂量掂量。这号人物，不可不防。”

马拉柳没有应声。他和基特利一样，一句道别的话也没说便走了，科瑟斯通又一次独自留了下来。

## III. Murder / 三　谋杀

===Original===
When Mallalieu had gone, Cotherstone gathered up the papers which his clerk had brought in, and sitting down at his desk tried to give his attention to them. The effort was not altogether a success. He had hoped that the sharing of the bad news with his partner would bring some relief to him, but his anxieties were still there. He was always seeing that queer, sinister look in Kitely’s knowing eyes: it suggested that as long as Kitely lived there would be no safety. Even if Kitely kept his word, kept any compact made with him, he would always have the two partners under his thumb. And for thirty years Cotherstone had been under no man’s thumb, and the fear of having a master was hateful to him. He heartily wished that Kitely was dead—dead and buried, and his secret with him; he wished that it had been anywise possible to have crushed the life out of him where he sat in that easy chair as soon as he had shown himself the reptile that he was. A man might kill any poisonous insect, any noxious reptile at pleasure—why not a human bloodsucker like that?

He sat there a long time, striving to give his attention to his papers, and making a poor show of it. The figures danced about before him; he could make neither head nor tail of the technicalities in the specifications and estimates; every now and then fits of abstraction came over him, and he sat drumming the tips of his fingers on his blotting-pad, staring vacantly at the shadows in the far depths of the room, and always thinking—thinking of the terrible danger of revelation. And always, as an undercurrent, he was saying that for himself he cared naught—Kitely could do what he liked, or would have done what he liked, had there only been himself to think for. But—Lettie! All his life was now centred in her, and in her happiness, and Lettie’s happiness, he knew, was centred in the man she was going to marry. And Cotherstone, though he believed that he knew men pretty well, was not sure that he knew Windle Bent sufficiently to feel sure that he would endure a stiff test. Bent was ambitious—he was resolved on a career. Was he the sort of man to stand the knowledge which Kitely might give him? For there was always the risk that whatever he and Mallalieu might do, Kitely, while there was breath in him, might split.

A sudden ringing at the bell of the telephone in the outer office made Cotherstone jump in his chair as if the arresting hand of justice had suddenly been laid on him. In spite of himself he rose trembling, and there were beads of perspiration on his forehead as he walked across the room.
===Chinese===
马拉柳（Mallalieu）走后，科瑟斯通（Cotherstone）把书记员送进来的那叠文件收拢到一起，在书桌前坐下，试着把心思放到上面去。这番努力并不算完全成功。他本指望把这坏消息说给合伙人听，自己能松一口气，可焦虑依旧盘踞在心头。他眼前老是浮现基特利（Kitely）那双精明眼睛里的古怪阴冷的神气：那神气分明在说，只要基特利活着一天，就一天没有安稳日子。即便基特利信守承诺、恪守与他们订下的任何协议，他也永远会把两个合伙人捏在掌心里。而科瑟斯通三十年来不曾受制于任何人，一想到要有个主子骑在头上，他就深恶痛绝。他衷心盼望基特利死了才好——死了、埋了，把秘密也一并带进坟墓；他甚至指望，当基特利在那把安乐椅里现出他那条毒虫的真面目时，当场就把他的性命掐掉该有多好。人可以随手拍死毒虫、碾死害兽——对那样一条人形吸血虫，为什么就不行呢？

他在那里坐了很久，竭力想把注意力放到文件上，却收效甚微。数字在他眼前跳来跳去；说明书和预算书里那些技术细节，他看得云里雾里；他不时陷入失神，指尖在吸墨纸垫上敲敲打打，茫然地瞪着房间深处的暗影，脑子里转个不停——转的全是秘密败露的可怕风险。而在这一切底下，还始终潜着一层念头：他自己是不在乎的——倘若只需为他自己打算，基特利爱怎么着就怎么着好了。可是——莱蒂（Lettie）！他如今全部的生活都系在她身上，系在她的幸福上，而莱蒂的幸福，他知道，全系在她要嫁的那个人身上。科瑟斯通自认阅人颇多，却不敢说对温德尔·本特（Windle Bent）了解得足够深，深到可以断定此人经得起一场严酷的考验。本特（Bent）野心勃勃——他一心要闯出一番事业。他是那种承受得住基特利可能摊给他的底细的人吗？因为无论他和马拉柳如何应对，只要基特利还剩一口气，就始终有他把事情捅出去的风险。

外间办公室里电话铃骤然响起，惊得科瑟斯通在椅子里一跳，仿佛司法缉拿之手猝然搭上了他的肩头。他不由自主地颤抖着站起身，穿过房间时，额头上已沁出了汗珠。

===Original===
“Nerves!” he muttered to himself. “I must be in a queer way to be taken like that. It won’t do!—especially at this turn. What is it?” he demanded, going to the telephone. “Who is that?”

His daughter’s voice, surprised and admonitory, came to him along the wire.

“Is that you, father?” she exclaimed. “What are you doing? Don’t you remember you asked Windle, and his friend Mr. Brereton, to supper at eight o’clock. It’s a quarter to eight now. Do come home!”

Cotherstone let out an exclamation which signified annoyance. The event of the late afternoon had completely driven it out of his recollection that Windle Bent had an old schoolfriend, a young barrister from London, staying with him, and that both had been asked to supper that evening at Cotherstone’s house. But Cotherstone’s annoyance was not because of his own forgetfulness, but because his present abstraction made him dislike the notion of company.

“I’d forgotten—for the moment,” he called. “I’ve been very busy. All right, Lettie—I’m coming on at once. Shan’t be long.”
===Chinese===
“神经出毛病了！”他喃喃自语，“我一定是状态不对劲，才会这样失魂落魄。这可不行！——眼下这个节骨眼上尤其不行。什么事？”他走到电话跟前问，“你是哪位？”

电话线那头传来他女儿的声音，又是惊讶，又是嗔怪。

“是您吗，爸爸？”她喊道，“您在干什么呀？您不记得自己请了温德尔和他朋友布里尔顿（Brereton）先生今晚八点来吃晚饭吗？现在已经七点三刻了。快回来吧！”

科瑟斯通脱口发出一声表示恼火的感叹。傍晚那桩事让他把一件安排忘了个干净：温德尔·本特有位老同学——一个从伦敦（London）来的年轻出庭律师——正在他家做客，两人今晚都被请到科瑟斯通家里吃晚饭。不过科瑟斯通恼的倒不是自己健忘，而是眼下心绪不宁，实在不想招待客人。

“一时忘了，”他冲着话筒说，“我这儿忙得不可开交。好啦，莱蒂——我马上动身。用不了多久。”

===Original===
But when he had left the telephone he made no haste. He lingered by his desk; he was slow in turning out the gas; slow in quitting and locking up his office; he went slowly away through the town. Nothing could have been further from his wishes than a desire to entertain company that night—and especially a stranger. His footsteps dragged as he passed through the marketplace and turned into the outskirts beyond.

Some years previously to this, when they had both married and made money, the two partners had built new houses for themselves. Outside Highmarket, on its western boundary, rose a long, low hill called Highmarket Shawl; the slope which overhung the town was thickly covered with fir and pine, amidst which great masses of limestone crag jutted out here and there. At the foot of this hill, certain plots of building land had been sold, and Mallalieu had bought one and Cotherstone another, and on these they had erected two solid stone houses, fitted up with all the latest improvements known to the building trade. Each was proud of his house; each delighted in welcoming friends and acquaintances there—this was the first night Cotherstone could remember on which it was hateful to him to cross his own threshold. The lighted windows, the smell of good things cooked for supper, brought him no sense of satisfaction; he had to make a distinct effort to enter and to present a face of welcome to his two guests, who were already there, awaiting him.

“Couldn’t get in earlier,” he said, replying to Lettie’s half-anxious, half-playful scoldings. “There was some awkward business turned up this evening—and as it is, I shall have to run away for an hour after supper—can’t be helped. How do you do, sir?” he went on, giving his hand to the stranger. “Glad to see you in these parts—you’ll find this a cold climate after London, I’m afraid.”
===Chinese===
可他放下电话之后却并不着急。他在书桌旁磨蹭；关煤气灯磨磨蹭蹭；离开办公室、锁门也慢慢吞吞；穿过镇子时，步子依旧迟缓。那天夜里他最不愿做的就是招待客人——尤其是招待一个生人。他拖着脚步走过集市广场，拐进了另一头的城郊。

早几年，两个合伙人各自成了家、也有了钱，便给自己盖起了新宅。海马基特（Highmarket）镇外西陲，隆起一道绵长低矮的山丘，名叫海马基特肖山（Highmarket Shawl）；俯临镇子的那面山坡长满茂密的冷杉和松树，其间到处凸出一大块一大块的石灰岩峭壁。山脚下有几块宅地出售，马拉柳买下一块，科瑟斯通买下另一块，两人各自盖起一幢结实的石头宅子，配置了当时建筑业所知的种种最新式改良设施。两人都以自己的宅子为荣，都乐意在那儿招待亲友故旧——而这是科瑟斯通记忆中头一个跨自家门槛竟感到厌恶的夜晚。亮着灯的窗户、为晚饭烹制的可口饭菜的香气，没有给他带来丝毫快意；他不得不强打起精神走进门去，对已经等在屋里的两位客人摆出一副欢迎的面孔。

“没法更早脱身啊，”他一面回应着莱蒂半是着急、半是打趣的埋怨，一面说，“今晚出了点棘手的事——而且这样吧，吃完晚饭我还得跑出去一个钟头——实在没法子。您好啊，先生，”他接着说，向那位生客伸出手，“很高兴您到我们这地方来——就是怕您受惯了伦敦的气候，要嫌这儿冷了。”

===Original===
He took a careful look at Bent’s friend as they all sat down to supper—out of sheer habit of inspecting any man who was new to him. And after a glance or two he said to himself that this young limb of the law was a sharp chap—a keen-eyed, alert, noticeable fellow, whose every action and tone denoted great mental activity. He was sharper than Bent, said Cotherstone, and in his opinion, that was saying a good deal. Bent’s ability was on the surface; he was an excellent specimen of the business man of action, who had ideas out of the common but was not so much given to deep and quiet thinking as to prompt doing of things quickly decided on. He glanced from one to the other, mentally comparing them. Bent was a tall, handsome man, blonde, blue-eyed, ready of word and laugh; Brereton, a medium-sized, compact fellow, dark of hair and eye, with an olive complexion that almost suggested foreign origin: the sort, decided Cotherstone, that thought a lot and said little. And forcing himself to talk he tried to draw the stranger out, watching him, too, to see if he admired Lettie. For it was one of Cotherstone’s greatest joys in life to bring folk to his house and watch the effect which his pretty daughter had on them, and he was rewarded now in seeing that the young man from London evidently applauded his friend’s choice and paid polite tribute to Lettie’s charm.

“And what might you have been doing with Mr. Brereton since he got down yesterday?” asked Cotherstone. “Showing him round, of course?”

“I’ve been tormenting him chiefly with family history,” answered Bent, with a laughing glance at his sweetheart. “You didn’t know I was raking up everything I could get hold of about my forbears, did you? Oh, I’ve been busy at that innocent amusement for a month past—old Kitely put me up to it.”

Cotherstone could barely repress an inclination to start in his chair; he himself was not sure that he did not show undue surprise.

“What!” he exclaimed. “Kitely? My tenant? What does he know about your family? A stranger!”
===Chinese===
众人落座用晚饭时，他细细打量了本特的朋友一眼——纯粹是出于审视任何生人的老习惯。只瞥了一两眼，他便暗自断定，这个法律行的年轻后生是个厉害角色——目光锐利、机警醒目，一举一动、一言一语都透着脑子转得飞快。科瑟斯通心想，此人比本特还要精明——而照他看，这话说得可不小。本特的才干摆在明面上；他是实干型生意人的出色样板，点子不同凡响，但比起静水深流式的深思，他更擅长的是雷厉风行、说干就干。他在两人之间来回端详，心里暗暗比较。本特身材高大、相貌堂堂，金发碧眼，谈笑爽利；布里尔顿则是中等个子、结结实实，头发眼睛都是深色，一副橄榄色的面孔，几乎让人疑心他有外国血统：这种人，科瑟斯通断定，是想得多、说得少。他强逼着自己开口，设法引那生客说话，同时留意观察他是否倾慕莱蒂。因为把客人请到家里、再瞧瞧漂亮女儿在他们身上激起的反应，正是科瑟斯通平生一大乐事；此刻他便得了回报——只见这位伦敦来的年轻人显然赞许朋友的眼光，并对莱蒂的魅力礼数周全地称赏不已。

“布里尔顿先生昨天到镇上以后，你们俩都干了些什么呀？”科瑟斯通问，“当然是领着他到处逛了逛？”

“我主要是拿家族史折腾他，”本特答道，笑吟吟地瞟了他的未婚妻一眼，“您还不知道我正在把祖宗八代的事儿能刨到的全刨出来吧？噢，这桩清白的消遣我已经忙了一个月了——是老基特利撺掇我干的。”

科瑟斯通险些压不住在椅子里惊跳起来的冲动；连他自己也不敢说，脸上没有露出过分的惊讶。

“什么！”他失声道，“基特利？我那房客？他怎么会知道你们家的事？一个外乡人！”

===Original===
“Much more than I do,” replied Bent. “The old chap’s nothing to do, you know, and since he took up his abode here he’s been spending all his time digging up local records—he’s a good bit of an antiquary, and that sort of thing. The Town Clerk tells me Kitely’s been through nearly all the old town documents—chests full of them! And Kitely told me one day that if I liked he’d trace our pedigree back to I don’t know when, and as he seemed keen, I told him to go ahead. He’s found out a lot of interesting things in the borough records that I never heard of.”

Cotherstone had kept his eyes on his plate while Bent was talking; he spoke now without looking up.

“Oh?” he said, trying to speak unconcernedly. “Ah!—then you’ll have been seeing a good deal of Kitely lately?”

“Not so much,” replied Bent. “He’s brought me the result of his work now and then—things he’s copied out of old registers, and so on.”

“And what good might it all amount to?” asked Cotherstone, more for the sake of talking than for any interest he felt. “Will it come to aught?”
===Chinese===
“比我知道的多得多，”本特答道，“您知道，老爷子闲着没事，自打搬到这儿住，他就把工夫全花在扒掘地方旧档上了——他颇有几分古物学家的味道，就爱这一类事。市镇书记官（Town Clerk）告诉我，基特利几乎翻遍了镇上所有的老文档——满满几大箱呢！基特利有一天对我说，要是我乐意，他可以把我们家的世系一直追溯到不知哪年哪月；看他兴致那么高，我就让他放手去查了。他在市镇档案里翻出了好多我闻所未闻的趣事。”

本特说话的时候，科瑟斯通一直盯着自己面前的盘子；这会儿他开了口，眼睛也不抬。

“哦？”他说，竭力显得漫不经心，“啊！——这么说，你近来常见着基特利了？”

“倒也不算多，”本特答道，“他隔三差五把查出的成果给我送来——都是从旧簿册里抄录下来的东西，诸如此类。”

“这些到底能折腾出个什么名堂？”科瑟斯通问，多半是为了找话说，并非真有什么兴致，“能查出个所以然来吗？”

===Original===
“Bent wants to trace his family history back to the Conquest,” observed Brereton, slyly. “He thinks the original Bent came over with the Conqueror. But his old man hasn’t got beyond the Tudor period yet.”

“Never mind!” said Bent. “There were Bents in Highmarket in Henry the Seventh’s time, anyhow. And if one has a pedigree, why not have it properly searched out? He’s a keen old hand at that sort of thing, Kitely. The Town Clerk says he can read some of our borough charters of six hundred years ago as if they were newspaper articles.”

Cotherstone made no remark on that. He was thinking. So Kitely was in close communication with Bent, was he?—constantly seeing him, being employed by him? Well, that cut two ways. It showed that up to now he had taken no advantage of his secret knowledge and might therefore be considered as likely to play straight if he were squared by the two partners. But it also proved that Bent would probably believe anything that Kitely might tell him. Certainly Kitely must be dealt with at once. He knew too much, and was obviously too clever, to be allowed to go about unfettered. Cost what it might, he must be attached to the Mallalieu-Cotherstone interest. And what Cotherstone was concentrating on just then, as he ate and drank, was—how to make that attachment in such a fashion that Kitely would have no option but to keep silence. If only he and Mallalieu could get a hold on Kitely, such as that which he had on them—
===Chinese===
“本特想把家史一直追溯到诺曼征服呢，”布里尔顿狡黠地说，“他认定本特家的老祖宗是随征服者渡海过来的。可惜他那位老人家到现在还没查过都铎时期那一截。”

“那有什么要紧！”本特说，“不管怎么说，亨利七世那会儿，海马基特就有姓本特的人了。一个人既然有家谱，何不把它好好查个清楚？基特利干这个是把老手，劲头足着呢。市镇书记官说，我们市镇六百年前的特许状，他有些读起来就跟读报纸文章一样。”

科瑟斯通对此没有作声。他在心里盘算。这么说，基特利跟本特过从甚密喽？——时常见面，还替他办事？唔，这事有利也有弊。一方面，这说明他至今没有拿那桩秘密派过用场，因此可以认为，只要两位合伙人肯出钱收买，他多半会照规矩行事。可另一方面，这也证明，基特利无论对本特说什么，本特八成都会信。毫无疑问，基特利的事必须立刻料理。他知道得太多，人又显然太精明，绝不能放任他自由来去。无论付出什么代价，都得把他牢牢拴在马拉柳—科瑟斯通这条线上。而科瑟斯通此刻一边吃着喝着，心里专注盘算的是——怎样把这根拴绳系得让基特利除了闭嘴别无出路。但愿他和马拉柳能在基特利身上抓到个把柄，就像基特利抓着他们的一样——

===Original===
“Well,” he said as supper came to an end, “I’m sorry, but I’m forced to leave you gentlemen for an hour, at any rate—can’t be helped. Lettie, you must try to amuse ’em until I come back. Sing Mr. Brereton some of your new songs. Bent—you know where the whisky and the cigars are—help yourselves—make yourselves at home.”

“You won’t be more than an hour, father?” asked Lettie.

“An hour’ll finish what I’ve got to do,” replied Cotherstone, “maybe less—I’ll be as quick as I can, anyway, my lass.”

He hurried off without further ceremony; a moment later and he had exchanged the warmth and brightness of his comfortable dining-room for the chill night and the darkness. And as he turned out of his garden he was thinking still further and harder. So Windle Bent was one of those chaps who have what folk call family pride, was he? Actually proud of the fact that he had a pedigree, and could say who his grandfather and grandmother were?—things on which most people were as hazy as they were indifferent. In that case, if he was really family-proud, all the more reason why Kitely should be made to keep his tongue still. For if Windle Bent was going on the game of making out that he was a man of family, he certainly would not relish the prospect of uniting his ancient blood with that of a man who had seen the inside of a prison. Kitely!—promptly and definitely—and for *good*!—that was the ticket.
===Chinese===
“好了，”晚饭临近尾声时他说，“很抱歉，至少有一个钟头，我不得不撇下两位先生——实在没法子。莱蒂，你得想法子陪他们玩，等我回来。给布里尔顿先生唱唱你的新歌。本特——威士忌和雪茄搁在哪儿你是知道的——自管随便——就当在自己家里一样。”

“您不会去一个多钟头吧，爸爸？”莱蒂问。

“一个钟头就够我把事办完了，”科瑟斯通答道，“也许还用不了——反正我尽量快些，好姑娘。”

他不再多客气，匆匆出了门；片刻之间，温暖明亮的舒适餐厅便换成了寒夜与黑暗。走出园门时，他还在往深里想，越想越狠。这么说，温德尔·本特是那种讲究所谓门第之傲的家伙喽？居然以自己有家谱、说得出祖父祖母是谁为荣？——这种事多数人既茫然无知，也懒得多想。果真如此，倘若他当真爱惜门第，那就更有理由让基特利把嘴闭严实了。因为温德尔·本特既然打算玩这套标榜自家系出名门的把戏，他自然不会乐意把自己古老的血统，和一个坐过牢的人家结成姻亲。基特利！——要办就得快、就得绝——而且要一劳永逸！——这才是正经主意。

===Original===
Cotherstone went off into the shadows of the night—and a good hour had passed when he returned to his house. It was then ten o’clock; he afterwards remembered that he glanced at the old grandfather clock in his hall when he let himself in. All was very quiet in there; he opened the drawing-room door to find the two young men and Lettie sitting over a bright fire, and Brereton evidently telling the other two some story, which he was just bringing to a conclusion.

“… for it’s a fact, in criminal practice,” Brereton was saying, “that there are no end of undiscovered crimes—there are any amount of guilty men going about free as the air, and—”

“Hope you’ve been enjoying yourselves,” said Cotherstone, going forward to the group. “I’ve been as quick as I could.”

“Mr. Brereton has been telling us most interesting stories about criminals,” said Lettie. “Facts—much stranger than fiction!”

“Then I’m sure it’s time he’d something to refresh himself with,” said Cotherstone hospitably. “Come away, gentlemen, and we’ll see if we can’t find a drop to drink and a cigar to smoke.”
===Chinese===
科瑟斯通走进夜色深处——等他回到家里，一个多钟头已经过去了。那时是十点钟；他后来还记得，开门进屋时自己瞥了一眼门厅里的老式落地大摆钟。屋里静悄悄的；他推开客厅门，只见两位年轻人和莱蒂围坐在明亮的炉火边，布里尔顿显然正在给另外两人讲故事，恰好讲到收尾。

“……因为干刑事这一行的都知道，这是实情，”布里尔顿正说着，“天下没被侦破的罪行多得数不清——多少背着罪孽的人照样满世界逍遥，自在得跟空气一样，而且——”

“但愿你们玩得尽兴，”科瑟斯通说着向那三人走去，“我已经尽快赶回来了。”

“布里尔顿先生一直在给我们讲罪犯的故事，有趣极了，”莱蒂说，“都是真事——比小说还要离奇！”

“那我看，正该让他喝点什么提提神了，”科瑟斯通殷勤地说，“来吧，两位先生，瞧瞧我们能不能找出点酒喝、几支雪茄抽。”

===Original===
He led the way to the dining-room and busied himself in bringing out some boxes of cigars from a cupboard while Lettie produced decanters and glasses from the sideboard.

“So you’re interested in criminal matters, sir?” observed Cotherstone as he offered Brereton a cigar. “Going in for that line, eh?”

“What practice I’ve had has been in that line,” answered Brereton, with a quiet laugh. “One sort of gets pitchforked into these things, you know, so—”

“What’s that?” exclaimed Lettie, who was just then handing the young barrister a tumbler of whisky and soda which Bent had mixed for him. “Somebody running hurriedly up the drive—as if something had happened! Surely you’re not going to be fetched out again, father?”

A loud ringing of the bell prefaced the entrance of some visitor, whose voice was heard in eager conversation with a parlourmaid in the hall.

“That’s your neighbour—Mr. Garthwaite,” said Bent.
===Chinese===
他把两人领进餐厅，忙着从柜子里搬出几盒雪茄；莱蒂则从餐具柜里取出细颈酒瓶和玻璃杯。

“这么说，您对刑事案子有兴趣，先生？”科瑟斯通一面递雪茄给布里尔顿，一面搭话，“打算走这一行喽？”

“我经手的案子，倒都在这一行里，”布里尔顿浅浅一笑，答道，“您知道，人这一辈子总有些事是身不由己被卷进去的，所以——”

“那是什么声音？”莱蒂叫了起来。她正把本特调好的一杯威士忌苏打递给这位年轻出庭律师。“有人沿着车道急匆匆跑上来——像是出了什么事！爸爸，您总不至于又要被人叫走吧？”

一阵响亮的门铃过后，一位客人走了进来，门厅里传来他跟客厅女仆急切的说话声。

“那是您的邻居——加斯韦特（Garthwaite）先生，”本特说。

===Original===
Cotherstone set down the cigars and opened the dining-room door. A youngish, fresh-coloured man, who looked upset and startled, came out of the hall, glancing round him inquiringly.

“Sorry to intrude, Mr. Cotherstone,” he said. “I say!—that old gentleman you let the cottage to—Kitely, you know.”

“What of him?” demanded Cotherstone sharply.

“He’s lying there in the coppice above your house—I stumbled over him coming through there just now,” replied Garthwaite. “He—don’t be frightened, Miss Cotherstone—he’s—well, there’s no doubt of it—he’s dead! And—”

“And—what?” asked Cotherstone. “What, man? Out with it!”

“And I should say, murdered!” said Garthwaite. “I—yes, I just saw enough to say that. Murdered—without a doubt!”
===Chinese===
科瑟斯通放下雪茄，拉开了餐厅门。一个面色红润的年轻人从门厅里走出来，神色慌乱、一脸惊惶，进屋便四下张望。

“抱歉冒昧打扰，科瑟斯通先生，”他说，“我说！——就是您把村舍租给的那位老先生——基特利，您知道的。”

“他怎么了？”科瑟斯通厉声问。

“他躺在您家上头那片小树林里——我刚才打那儿穿过来，绊在他身上了，”加斯韦特答道，“他——您别害怕，科瑟斯通小姐——他——唉，错不了——他死了！还有——”

“还有——什么？”科瑟斯通问，“还有什么？说呀，伙计？快说出来！”

“我看哪，是被人谋害的！”加斯韦特说，“我——是的，我瞅见的那一眼，就够我这么说了。谋害——错不了！”

## IV. The Pine Wood / 四　松林

===Original===
Brereton, standing back in the room, the cigar which Cotherstone had just given him unlighted in one hand, the glass which Lettie had presented to him in the other, was keenly watching the man who had just spoken and the man to whom he spoke. But all his attention was quickly concentrated on Cotherstone. For despite a strong effort to control himself, Cotherstone swayed a little, and instinctively put out a hand and clutched Bent’s arm. He paled, too—the sudden spasm of pallor was almost instantly succeeded by a quick flush of colour. He made another effort—and tried to laugh.

“Nonsense, man!” he said thickly and hoarsely. “Murder? Who should want to kill an old chap like that? It’s—here, give me a drink, one of you—that’s—a bit startling!”

Bent seized a tumbler which he himself had just mixed, and Cotherstone gulped off half its contents. He looked round apologetically.

“I—I think I’m not as strong as I was,” he muttered. “Overwork, likely—I’ve been a bit shaky of late. A shock like that—”

“I’m sorry,” said Garthwaite, who looked surprised at the effect of his news. “I ought to have known better. But you see, yours is the nearest house—”

“Quite right, my lad, quite right,” exclaimed Cotherstone. “You did the right thing. Here!—we’d better go up. Have you called the police?”
===Chinese===
布里尔顿站在房间靠后的地方，一手拿着科瑟斯通刚递给他的雪茄，还没点燃，另一手端着莱蒂奉上的酒杯，正锐利地打量着刚开口说话的那个人和他说话的对象。但他的注意力很快便全落到了科瑟斯通身上。只见科瑟斯通尽管竭力克制自己，身子还是晃了一晃，本能地伸手抓住了本特的手臂。他的脸也白了——那阵突兀的苍白几乎转瞬便被一阵潮红取代。他又强打了一次精神——还想挤出笑来。

“胡说，伙计！”他声音喑哑含混地说道，“谋杀？谁会想要害死那么一个老家伙？这——喂，哪位给我倒杯酒——这可——叫人吃了一惊！”

本特抓过他自己刚调好的一杯酒，科瑟斯通咕咚咕咚灌下去半杯，随即带着歉意环视众人。

“我——我看我是不如从前结实了，”他喃喃道，“多半是操劳过度——近来我有点儿发虚。这么一下子惊吓——”

“真对不起，”加斯韦特说，他没料到自己的消息竟惹出这样的反应，“我本该想得周到些的。可您瞧，您家是离得最近的——”

“做得对，小伙子，做得对，”科瑟斯通连声说道，“你做得对。喂！——我们最好上去一趟。警察叫了吗？”

===Original===
“I sent the man from the cottage at the foot of your garden,” answered Garthwaite. “He was just locking up as I passed, so I told him, and sent him off.”

“We’ll go,” said Cotherstone. He looked round at his guests. “You’ll come?” he asked.

“Don’t you go, father,” urged Lettie, “if you’re not feeling well.”

“I’m all right,” insisted Cotherstone. “A mere bit of weakness—that’s all. Now that I know what’s to be faced—” he twisted suddenly on Garthwaite—“what makes you think it’s murder?” he demanded. “Murder! That’s a big word.”

Garthwaite glanced at Lettie, who was whispering to Bent, and shook his head.

“Tell you when we get outside,” he said. “I don’t want to frighten your daughter.”
===Chinese===
“我派您花园脚下那座村舍里的男人去了，”加斯韦特答道，“我路过时他正要锁门，我就把事情告诉他，打发他去了。”

“那我们就去，”科瑟斯通说。他环视两位客人，“二位愿意同去吗？”

“爸爸，您要是不舒服，就别去了，”莱蒂劝道。

“我没事，”科瑟斯通执意说道，“只是有点儿发虚——仅此而已。既然我知道要面对的是什么了——”他猛地转向加斯韦特——“你怎么就断定是谋杀？”他质问道，“谋杀！这可是个分量不轻的字眼。”

加斯韦特瞥了一眼正跟本特窃窃私语的莱蒂，摇了摇头。

“等出了门再告诉您，”他说，“我不想吓着令嫒。”

===Original===
“Come on, then,” said Cotherstone. He hurried into the hall and snatched up an overcoat. “Fetch me that lantern out of the kitchen,” he called to the parlourmaid. “Light it! Don’t you be afraid, Lettie,” he went on, turning to his daughter. “There’s naught to be afraid of—now. You gentlemen coming with us?”

Bent and Brereton had already got into their coats: when the maid came with the lantern, all four men went out. And as soon as they were in the garden Cotherstone turned on Garthwaite.

“How do you know he’s murdered?” he asked. “How could you tell?”

“I’ll tell you all about it, now we’re outside,” answered Garthwaite. “I’d been over to Spennigarth, to see Hollings. I came back over the Shawl, and made a shortcut through the wood. And I struck my foot against something—something soft, you know—I don’t like thinking of that! And so I struck a match, and looked, and saw this old fellow—don’t like thinking of that, either. He was laid there, a few yards out of the path that runs across the Shawl at that point. I saw he was dead—and as for his being murdered, well, all I can say is, he’s been strangled! That’s flat.”

“Strangled!” exclaimed Bent.

“Aye, without doubt,” replied Garthwaite. “There’s a bit of rope round his neck that tight that I couldn’t put my little finger between it and him! But you’ll see for yourselves—it’s not far up the Shawl. You never heard anything, Mr. Cotherstone?”

“No, we heard naught,” answered Cotherstone. “If it’s as you say, there’d be naught to hear.”
===Chinese===
“那就走吧，”科瑟斯通说。他匆匆跑进门厅，一把抓起大衣。“去厨房把那盏提灯给我拿来，”他朝客厅女仆喊道，“点上！别怕，莱蒂，”他接着转向女儿说，“没什么可怕的——眼下没有。两位先生跟我们一道去吗？”

本特和布里尔顿已经穿好了外套；女仆取来提灯后，四个男人便出了门。一走进花园，科瑟斯通就冲着加斯韦特发作了。

“你怎么知道他是被人谋害的？”他问，“你怎么看出来的？”

“这会儿到了外头，我就原原本本告诉您吧，”加斯韦特答道，“我刚才去斯佩尼加斯（Spennigarth），看霍林斯（Hollings）来着。回来时翻肖山，抄近路穿过那片树林。我的脚撞上了什么东西——软绵绵的东西，您知道——想起来我就不舒服！于是我划了根火柴，一看，就瞧见了这位老先生——这想起来也不好受。他躺在那儿，离从那儿横穿肖山的那条小径几码远。我一看他死了——至于说是谋害，唉，我只能说，他是被人勒死的！就这么回事。”

“勒死的！”本特失声叫道。

“是呀，错不了，”加斯韦特答道，“他脖子上勒着一截绳子，紧得我连小指头都塞不进绳子和脖子中间！反正你们自己会看到的——离肖山没多远。科瑟斯通先生，您没听见什么动静吧？”

“没有，我们什么也没听见，”科瑟斯通答道，“要是真像你说的那样，本来也不会有什么动静。”

===Original===
He had led them out of his grounds by a side-gate, and they were now in the thick of the firs and pines which grew along the steep, somewhat rugged slope of the Shawl. He put the lantern into Garthwaite’s hand.

“Here—you show the way,” he said. “I don’t know where it is, of course.”

“You were going straight to it,” remarked Garthwaite. He turned to Brereton, who was walking at his side. “You’re a lawyer, aren’t you?” he asked. “I heard that Mr. Bent had a lawyer friend stopping with him just now—we hear all the bits of news in a little place like Highmarket. Well—you’ll understand, likely—it hadn’t been long done!”

“You noticed that?” said Brereton.

“I touched him,” replied Garthwaite. “His hand and cheek were—just warm. He couldn’t have been dead so very long—as I judged matters. And—here he is!”
===Chinese===
他领着众人从边门出了自家庭院，此刻他们已置身于肖山陡峭而有些崎岖的山坡上密密丛生的冷杉和松树之间。他把提灯塞到加斯韦特手里。

“拿着——你来带路，”他说，“我当然不知道人在哪儿。”

“您刚才正好是直冲着那儿走呢，”加斯韦特说。他转向走在身旁的布里尔顿。“您是位律师，对吧？”他问，“我听说本特先生眼下有位律师朋友住在他那儿——海马基特这么个小地方，什么零零碎碎的消息都传得到我们耳朵里。这个——您大概明白——案发时间不长！”

“这您也看出来了？”布里尔顿说。

“我碰过他，”加斯韦特答道，“他的手和脸颊——还温着呢。他死了不至于多久——我是这么判断的。瞧——他在这儿！”

===Original===
He twisted sharply round the corner of one of the great masses of limestone which cropped out amongst the trees, and turned the light of the lantern on the dead man.

“There!” he said in a hushed voice. “There!”

The four men came to a halt, each gazing steadily at the sight they had come to see. It needed no more than a glance to assure each that he was looking on death: there was that in Kitely’s attitude which forbade any other possibility.

“He’s just as I found him,” whispered Garthwaite. “I came round this rock from there, d’ye see, and my foot knocked against his shoulder. But, you know, he’s been dragged here! Look at that!”

Brereton, after a glance at the body, had looked round at its surroundings. The wood thereabouts was carpeted—thickly carpeted—with pine needles; they lay several inches thick beneath the trunks of the trees; they stretched right up to the edge of the rock. And now, as Garthwaite turned the lantern, they saw that on this soft carpet there was a great slur—the murderer had evidently dragged his victim some yards across the pine needles before depositing him behind the rock. And at the end of this mark there were plain traces of a struggle—the soft, easily yielding stuff was disturbed, kicked about, upheaved, but as Brereton at once recognized, it was impossible to trace footprints in it.

“That’s where it must have been,” said Garthwaite. “You see there’s a bit of a path there. The old man must have been walking along that path, and whoever did it must have sprung out on him there—where all those marks are—and when he’d strangled him dragged him here. That’s how I figure it, Mr. Cotherstone.”
===Chinese===
他猛地绕过树丛间露出地面的一大块石灰岩，把提灯的光照向死者。

“喏！”他压低声音说，“喏！”

四个人停住脚步，各自定睛望着他们专程前来看的这番景象。只消一眼，每个人都确信自己面对的是死亡：基特利那副姿势里有一种东西，容不得任何别的解释。

“他就是我发现的那个样子，”加斯韦特低声说，“我是从那边绕过这块岩石的，明白吗，脚踢在了他肩膀上。可是你们瞧，他是被拖到这儿来的！看那个！”

布里尔顿对尸体看了一眼之后，便环顾起四周来。那一带的林地像地毯似的铺着松针——铺得厚厚的；树干底下的松针积了好几寸厚，一直延伸到岩石边缘。这时，随着加斯韦特转动提灯，他们看见这张柔软的地毯上有一道长长的拖痕——凶手显然是把被害人拖过好几码远的松针，才把他放置在岩石后面的。拖痕的尽头有明显的挣扎痕迹——松软易陷的东西被搅乱、踢散、翻起，但布里尔顿一眼就看出，在这种地面上根本无从辨认脚印。

“案发准是在那儿，”加斯韦特说，“你们看，那儿有段小径。老头儿准是正沿着那条小径走，下手的人准是在那儿向他扑过去的——就是满是痕迹的那块地方——勒死他之后，再把他拖到这儿来。科瑟斯通先生，我就是这么琢磨的。”

===Original===
Lights were coming up through the wood beneath them, glancing from point to point amongst the trees. Then followed a murmur of voices, and three or four men came into view—policemen, carrying their lamps, the man whom Garthwaite had sent into the town, and a medical man who acted as police surgeon.

“Here!” said Bent, as the newcomers advanced and halted irresolutely. “This way, doctor—there’s work for you here—of a sort, anyway. Of course, he’s dead?”

The doctor had gone forward as soon as he caught sight of the body, and he dropped on his knees at its side while the others gathered round. In the added light everybody now saw things more clearly. Kitely lay in a heap—just as a man would lie who had been unceremoniously thrown down. But Brereton’s sharp eyes saw at once that after he had been flung at the foot of the mass of rock some hand had disarranged his clothing. His overcoat and under coat had been torn open, hastily, if not with absolute violence; the lining of one trousers pocket was pulled out; there were evidences that his waistcoat had been unbuttoned and its inside searched: everything seemed to indicate that the murderer had also been a robber.

“He’s not been dead very long,” said the doctor, looking up. “Certainly not more than three-quarters of an hour. Strangled? Yes!—and by somebody who has more than ordinary knowledge of how quickly a man may be killed in that way! Look how this cord is tied—no amateur did that.”

He turned back the neckcloth from the dead man’s throat, and showed the others how the cord had been slipped round the neck in a running-knot and fastened tightly with a cunning twist.

“Whoever did this had done the same thing before—probably more than once,” he continued. “No man with that cord round his neck, tightly knotted like that, would have a chance—however free his hands might be. He’d be dead before he could struggle. Does no one know anything about this? No more than that?” he went on, when he had heard what Garthwaite could tell. “Well, this is murder, anyway! Are there no signs of anything about here?”
===Chinese===
树下的林间有灯火往上来，在树木之间闪烁游移。接着传来一阵人语声，三四个男人出现在眼前——提着灯的警察、加斯韦特打发进城报信的那个男人，还有一位兼任警方法医的医生。

“这边！”本特朝来人喊道，他们迟疑不决地走上前来又停住了。“这边，医生——这儿有您的活儿——不管怎么说，总有一样。人当然是死了吧？”

医生一看见尸体就抢步上前，在尸身旁跪下，其余的人围拢过来。添了灯光，众人此刻都看得更清楚了。基特利蜷作一堆躺着——就像被人随手撂倒的样子。但布里尔顿锐利的眼睛立刻看出，他被摔在那大块岩石脚下之后，有一只手翻乱过他的衣裳。大衣和里面的上衣被扯开了，纵然说不上用蛮力，至少十分仓促；一条裤袋的袋布被翻了出来；背心显然被解开过、里面也被搜过：一切都表明，凶手还兼做强盗。

“他死了还不久，”医生抬头说道，“肯定不超过三刻钟。勒死的？没错！——下手的人对用这种法子杀人能有多快，可远不止寻常的了解！看这绳子是怎么系的——外行干不出这个。”

他翻开死者喉部的围脖，指给大家看那根绳索是怎样用活结套上脖子、又用一个巧妙的旋拧紧紧扣死的。

“干这事的人以前就干过同样的勾当——多半还不止一回，”他接着说，“脖子上套着那样一根绳、打得那样紧的死结，任谁也没有活路——哪怕双手完全自由。不等挣扎，人就没气了。这事有人知道什么吗？就知道这些？”他听加斯韦特讲完，又往下问，“好吧，反正这是谋杀！这附近没有什么别的痕迹吗？”

===Original===
“Don’t you think his clothing looks as if he had been robbed?” said Brereton, pointing to the obvious signs. “That should be noted before he’s moved.”

“I’ve noted that, sir,” said the police-sergeant, who had bent over the body while the doctor was examining it. “There’s one of his pockets turned inside out, and all his clothing’s been torn open. Robbery, of course—that’s what it’s been—murder for the sake of robbery!”

One of the policemen, having satisfied his curiosity stepped back and began to search the surroundings with the aid of his lamp. He suddenly uttered a sharp exclamation.

“Here’s something!” he said, stooping to the foot of a pine-tree and picking up a dark object. “An old pocketbook—nothing in it, though.”

“That was his,” remarked Cotherstone. “I’ve seen it before. He used to carry it in an inner pocket. Empty, do you say?—no papers?”

“Not a scrap of anything,” answered the policeman, handing the book over to his sergeant, and proceeding to search further. “We’d best to see if there’s any footprints about.”
===Chinese===
“您不觉得他这身衣裳看上去像遭过劫吗？”布里尔顿指着明摆着的痕迹说，“搬动之前，这一点应该记下来。”

“我记下了，先生，”警佐说。医生验尸时，他一直在俯身查看尸体。“他有一只口袋被翻了个里朝外，全身衣裳都被扯开了。当然是劫财——就是这么回事——为了劫财而杀人！”

一名警察看足了好奇，退后几步，借着手里的提灯搜查起四周来。忽然，他发出一声短促的惊呼。

“这儿有东西！”他说着，俯身到一棵松树底下，捡起一件黑乎乎的东西，“一个旧皮夹——不过里头什么也没有。”

“那是他的东西，”科瑟斯通说，“我从前见过。他往常把它揣在贴身的内袋里。您是说，空的？——一张纸片也没有？”

“什么都没有，连个纸角都没有，”警察答道，一面把皮夹递给警佐，一面继续搜查，“咱们最好看看四周有没有脚印。”

===Original===
“You’d better examine that path, then,” said Garthwaite. “You’ll find no prints on all this pine-needle stuff—naught to go by, anyway—it’s too thick and soft. But he must have come along that path, one way or another—I’ve met him walking in here of an evening, more than once.”

The doctor, who had exchanged a word or two with the sergeant, turned to Cotherstone.

“Wasn’t he a tenant of yours?” he asked. “Had the cottage at the top of the Shawl here. Well, we’d better have the body removed there, and someone should go up and warn his family.”

“There’s no family,” answered Cotherstone. “He’d naught but a housekeeper—Miss Pett. She’s an elderly woman—and not likely to be startled, from what I’ve seen of her.”

“I’ll go,” said Bent. “I know the housekeeper.” He touched Brereton’s elbow, and led him away amongst the trees and up the wood. “This is a strange affair!” he continued when they were clear of the others. “Did you hear what Dr. Rockcliffe said?—that whoever had done it was familiar with that sort of thing!”

“I saw for myself,” replied Brereton. “I noticed that cord, and the knot on it, at once. A man whose neck was tied up like that could be thrown down, thrown anywhere, left to stand up, if you like, and he’d be literally helpless, even if, as the doctor said, he had the use of his hands. He’d be unconscious almost at once—dead very soon afterwards. Murder?—I should think so!—and a particularly brutal and determined one. Bent!—whoever killed that poor old fellow was a man of great strength and of—knowledge! Knowledge, mind you!—he knew the trick. You haven’t any doubtful character in Highmarket who has ever lived in India, have you?”
===Chinese===
“那你们最好查查那条小径，”加斯韦特说，“这一片松针上你们找不出脚印——反正无从下手——太厚太软了。可他不管怎么走，一定是打那条小径来的——我不止一回在傍晚撞见他在这一带散步。”

医生跟警佐交谈了一两句之后，转向科瑟斯通。

“他不是您的房客吗？”他问，“住的是这儿肖山顶上那座村舍。这样吧，我们最好把遗体移到那边去，还得有人上去给他家里人报个信。”

“没有什么家里人，”科瑟斯通答道，“他只有一个管家——佩特小姐（Miss Pett）。她上了年纪——照我看，不是个轻易会惊慌的人。”

“我去吧，”本特说，“我认识那位管家。”他碰了碰布里尔顿的臂肘，领着他穿过树丛、往林子上头走去。“这事真蹊跷！”待到离开了旁人，他接着说，“罗克利夫（Rockcliffe）医生的话你听见了吗？——说干这事的人是这一行的熟手！”

“我自己也看出来了，”布里尔顿答道，“那根绳子和上面的绳结，我一眼就注意到了。脖子被那样捆住的人，任凭你把他撂倒、扔到随便什么地方、随你让他自己站着，他都全然无能为力——即便像医生说的那样，他双手还能使唤。他几乎立刻就会失去知觉——很快就死。谋杀？——我看是！——而且是一桩格外残忍、蓄意十足的谋杀。本特！——害死那可怜老家伙的，是个力气极大、而且——有门道的人！是门道，你听着！——他懂这一行的诀窍。海马基特没有住过印度的什么形迹可疑的人物吧？”

===Original===
“India! Why India?” asked Bent.

“Because I should say that the man who did that job has learned some of the Indian tricks with cords and knots,” answered Brereton. “That murder’s suggestive of Thuggeeism in some respects. That the cottage?” he went on, pointing to a dim light ahead of him. “This housekeeper, now?—is she the sort who’ll take it quietly?”

“She’s as queer a character as the old fellow himself was,” replied Bent, as they cleared the wood and entered a hedge-enclosed garden at the end of which stood an old-fashioned cottage. “I’ve talked to her now and then when calling here—I should say she’s a woman of nerve.”

Brereton looked narrowly at Miss Pett when she opened the door. She carried a tallow candle in one hand and held it high above her head to throw a light on the callers; its dim rays fell more on herself than on them. A tall, gaunt, elderly woman, almost fleshless of face, and with a skin the colour of old parchment, out of which shone a pair of bright black eyes; the oddity of her appearance was heightened by her headdress—a glaring red and yellow handkerchief tightly folded in such a fashion as to cover any vestige of hair. Her arms, bare to the elbow, and her hands were as gaunt as her face, but Brereton was quick to recognize the suggestion of physical strength in the muscles and sinews under the parchment-like skin. A strange, odd-looking woman altogether, he thought, and not improved by the fact that she appeared to have lost all her teeth, and that a long, sharp nose and prominent chin almost met before her sunken lips.

“Oh, it’s you, is it, Mr. Bent?” she said, before either of the young men could speak. “Mr. Kitely’s gone out for his regular bedtime constitution—he will have that, wet or fine, every night. But he’s much longer than usual, and—”

She stopped suddenly, seeing some news in Bent’s face, and her own contracted to a questioning look.
===Chinese===
“印度！怎么扯到印度？”本特问。

“因为照我看，干这活的人学过印度人玩绳子和绳结的一些门道，”布里尔顿答道，“这桩谋杀案在某些方面让人想起印度的图吉教（Thuggeeism）做派。那就是那座村舍？”他指着前方一点昏暗的灯光，继续说道，“那么这位管家呢？——她是能沉得住气的那种人吗？”

“她是个跟那老家伙一样古怪的人物，”本特答道。这时他们已走出树林，进了一个四面围着树篱的园子，园子尽头立着一座老式村舍。“我来这儿做客的时候，偶尔跟她聊过几句——我看她是个有胆量的女人。”

佩特小姐开门时，布里尔顿细细打量了她。她一手擎着一支牛油蜡烛，高举过头顶，好照亮来客；烛光昏暗，落在她自己身上的反倒比落在来客身上的多。一个瘦高干瘪的老妇人，脸几乎没有肉，皮肤是陈年羊皮纸的颜色，从中透出一双乌亮乌亮的眼睛；她的包头头巾更添了几分古怪——一条扎眼的红黄相间的手帕，缠得紧紧的，把头发遮了个一丝不剩。她赤裸到肘部的双臂和一双手跟她那张脸一样干瘦，但布里尔顿立刻看出，羊皮纸似的皮肤底下，筋骨间蕴着一股气力。总的说来，是个模样古怪的怪女人，他想；而她那口牙似乎掉了个精光，尖尖的长鼻子和突出的下巴几乎在瘪陷的嘴唇前碰到一处，这也无补于她的相貌。

“哦，是您啊，本特先生？”还不等两个年轻人开口，她就先说了，“基特利先生照例出去做睡前散步了——他每晚必得走这一趟，刮风下雨也不改。可他今晚比平常久得多，再说——”

她猛地顿住了，从本特脸上看出有什么消息，自己的脸也绷了起来，露出探问的神色。

===Original===
“Is there aught amiss?” she asked. “Has something happened him? Aught that’s serious? You needn’t be afraid to speak, Mr. Bent—there’s naught can upset or frighten me, let me tell you—I’m past all that!”

“I’m afraid Mr. Kitely’s past everything, too, then,” said Bent. He looked steadily at her for a moment, and seeing that she understood, went on. “They’re bringing him up, Miss Pett—you’d better make ready. You won’t be alarmed—I don’t think there’s any doubt that he’s been murdered.”

The woman gazed silently at her visitors; then, nodding her turbaned head, she drew back into the cottage.

“It’s what I expected,” she muttered. “I warned him—more than once. Well—let them bring him, then.”

She vanished into a side-room, and Bent and Brereton went down the garden and met the others, carrying the dead man. Cotherstone followed behind the police, and as he approached Bent he pulled him by the sleeve and drew him aside.

“There’s a clue!” he whispered. “A clue, d’ye hear—a strong clue!”
===Chinese===
“出事了吗？”她问，“他遭了什么事？要紧的事？您尽管直说，不必顾忌，本特先生——我跟您说，天底下没有什么能搅得乱我、吓得着我——我早过了那一步了！”

“那我看，基特利先生这回可是什么都过去了，”本特说。他定定地望了她片刻，见她已经明白，便继续说道，“他们这就把他抬上来，佩特小姐——您最好预备一下。您不会惊慌的——依我看，他是被人谋害的，这一点毫无疑问。”

老妇人默默瞪视着两位来客；随后，她那包着头巾的脑袋点了点，退进村舍里去。

“我早料到会这样，”她咕哝道，“我警告过他——不止一回。好吧——那就让他们把他抬回来吧。”

她闪身进了侧屋，不见了。本特和布里尔顿顺园子走下去，迎上了抬着死者的众人。科瑟斯通跟在警察后面，走近本特身边时，他扯了扯他的衣袖，把他拉到一旁。

“有线索了！”他压着嗓子说，“有线索，你听见没有——一条很有分量的线索！”

## V. The Cord / 五　绳索

===Original===

Ever since they had left the house at the foot of the pine wood, Brereton had been conscious of a curious psychological atmosphere, centring in Cotherstone. It had grown stronger as events had developed; it was still stronger now as they stood outside the dead man’s cottage, the light from the open door and the white-curtained window falling on Cotherstone’s excited face. Cotherstone, it seemed to Brereton, was unduly eager about something﻿—he might almost be said to be elated. All of his behaviour was odd. He had certainly been shocked when Garthwaite burst in with the news﻿—but this shock did not seem to be of the ordinary sort. He had looked like fainting﻿—but when he recovered himself his whole attitude (so, at any rate, it had seemed to Brereton) had been that of a man who has just undergone a great relief. To put the whole thing into a narrow compass, it seemed as if Cotherstone appeared to be positively pleased to hear﻿—and to find beyond doubt﻿—that Kitely was dead. And now, as he stood glancing from one young man to the other, his eyes glittered as if he were absolutely enjoying the affair: he reminded Brereton of that type of theatregoer who will insist on pointing out stage effects as they occur before his eyes, forcing his own appreciation of them upon fellow-watchers whose eyes are as keen as his own.

“A strong clue!” repeated Cotherstone, and said it yet again. “A good ’un! And if it’s right, it’ll clear matters up.”

“What is it?” asked Bent. He, too, seemed to be conscious that there was something odd about his prospective father-in-law, and he was gazing speculatively at him as if in wonder. “What sort of a clue?”

===Chinese===

打从离开松林脚下那座宅子起，布里尔顿便一直察觉到一种怪异的心理气氛，而这气氛正以科瑟斯通为中心。随着事态一步步展开，它越来越浓；此刻他们站在死者的小屋外，敞开的房门和挂着白窗帘的窗子透出的光落在科瑟斯通那张兴奋的脸上，气氛便愈发浓了。在布里尔顿看来，科瑟斯通对某件事热心得过了头——简直可以说是一副扬扬得意之态。他的举止从头到尾都很反常。加斯韦特闯进来报信时，他着实吃了一惊——可那一惊似乎又不是寻常的那一种。他当时看样子像要晕倒——可等他缓过神来，他整个人的姿态（至少在布里尔顿眼里是如此）却像一个刚刚如释重负的人。把整件事归结成一句话：科瑟斯通听说基特利死了——而且确凿无疑就是死了——竟像是当真心怀欢喜。而此刻，他站在那儿，目光在两个年轻人之间来回逡巡，双眼灼灼放光，仿佛全然在享受这桩事：这叫布里尔顿想起那种看戏的非要把眼前出现的舞台效果一一指给人看不可的观众，硬要把自己的一番领会强加给眼力同他一样尖利的同场看客。

“一条过硬的线索！”科瑟斯通念叨了一遍，又念叨了一遍。“真是条好的！要是没错，事情就能弄个水落石出了。”

“是什么呀？”本特问。他似乎也察觉这位未来的岳父有什么地方不对劲，正若有所思地盯着他，一脸纳闷。“什么样的线索？”

===Original===

“It’s a wonder it didn’t strike me﻿—and you, too﻿—at first,” said Cotherstone, with a queer sound that was half a chuckle. “But as long as it’s struck somebody, eh? One’s as good as another. You can’t think of what it is, now?”

“I don’t know what you’re thinking about,” replied Bent, half impatiently.

Cotherstone gave vent to an unmistakable chuckle at that, and he motioned them to follow him into the cottage.

“Come and see for yourselves, then,” he said. “You’ll spot it. But, anyway﻿—Mr. Brereton, being a stranger, can’t be expected to.”

===Chinese===

“怪就怪在起先我怎么没想到——你们俩也没想到，”科瑟斯通说，喉咙里发出一声怪响，半似窃笑。“不过只要有一个人想到了，嗯？谁想到还不都一样？你们这会儿还猜不着是什么吧？”

“我根本不知道您在想什么，”本特半带不耐烦地答道。

听他这么说，科瑟斯通索性笑出声来——那分明是一声窃笑——随即招手叫两人跟他进小屋。

“那你们就自己来瞧吧，”他说。“你们一眼就能看出毛病在哪儿。不过嘛——布里尔顿先生是个外乡人，看不出也不怪他。”

===Original===

The three men walked into the living-room of the cottage﻿—a good-sized, open-raftered, old-fashioned place, wherein burnt a bright fire, at either side of which stood two comfortable armchairs.
